# Conveyor batch B-093: context (generated 2026-11-24T03:17:44+00:00)

Write 10 families with ids E-928, E-929, E-930, E-931, E-932, E-933, E-934, E-935, E-936, E-937.

## Fixed for every family
- Markets ['WIN', 'WDO'], timeframes [1, 5] minutes; split: EXPLORATION only.
- Default exit (you do not write it): target R$30, stop R$30, time limit 30 min, replayed on 1-minute bars; entry at the open of the minute after the signal bar's close; one position at a time; at most 10 entries per session.
- Contracts: WIN tick 5 points = R$1; WDO tick 0.5 point = R$5. R$30 is 150 WIN points (30 ticks) but only 6 WDO ticks, and the WDO round-trip cost is about R$6, so WDO families need a move far larger than WIN's to survive this exit: give `markets=("WIN",)` unless the idea is about the dollar.
- Screen: >= 500 trades/year; mean net per trade > R$0 at the measured cost; >= 5 of 8 time slices positive; plateau: >= 2/3 of one-step neighbours positive; best variant per family; overlap <= 0.5 with rules already selected.
- Checks before any result: no look-ahead (prefix cuts) and the 21-session window.

Hypotheses of the screened families (do not repeat them):

- E-001 VWAP reclaim: After the price stays on one side of the session VWAP for a while, the first close back across it marks a change of control; follow the cross.
- E-002 Previous-day high/low rejection: Trading beyond the previous session's high/low and closing back inside it is a failed auction at a level many participants watch; fade it.
- E-003 Previous-day high/low breakout: A close beyond the previous session's high/low triggers stops and breakout orders; follow it.
- E-004 Trend-day pullback to the EMA: On a day already moving more than usual for the time of day, the first pullback to a short EMA that closes back on the trend side is a continuation entry.
- E-005 Inside-bar breakout: A bar inside the previous one compresses the range; a close beyond it releases it in that direction.
- E-006 Opening-range failure: When the price breaks the opening range and then closes back inside it, the breakout traders are trapped; fade back into the range.
- E-007 Quiet continuation: After a large move, a bar in the move's direction on LOW volume means nobody is pushing back; follow.
- E-008 Midday extreme fade: Between 12:00 and 14:30 liquidity is thinner and breaks of the recent high/low tend to fail; fade a bar that pokes beyond and closes back inside.
- E-009 Pin-bar rejection: A bar of at least typical range with a small body and a long wick on one side shows that aggressive orders pushed the price there and were absorbed by resting liquidity; the absorbed side is exhausted, follow the close away from the wick.
- E-010 Strong-close bar follow: A bar of typical-or-larger range that is almost all body and closes at its extreme means aggressive orders were still being filled at the bell of the bar; a parent order still being worked continues into the next bars, so follow it.
- E-011 Break-and-retest: After a close beyond the recent high/low, a pullback that touches the broken level and closes back on the breakout side shows old resistance acting as support (late sellers covering, missed buyers stepping in); enter on the retest rather than the break.
- E-012 Close-location flow divergence: A new recent low while the volume-weighted close location of the same window is positive means bars keep closing high on volume: passive buyers absorb the sellers making the low; fade the extreme.
- E-013 Session extreme after a pause: When the session high/low has stood untouched for a long time, stops and breakout orders pile up beyond it; the first close through it after the pause releases them, follow the break.
- E-014 Engulfing reversal after a run: After several consecutive closes in one direction, a bar that reverses and closes beyond the previous bar's extreme shows the short-term momentum traders are trapped and the other side took control; follow the reversal.
- E-015 Late-day trend breakout: Late-informed traders and rebalancers finish their orders near the close in the direction of the day; in the last hour or two, on a day already moving more than usual, follow a break of the 15-minute high/low in the day's direction.
- E-016 Double test that holds: A return to the recent low (high) after moving away, that does not break it and closes in the bounce direction, shows resting orders still defend the level and sellers cannot push through; trade the hold.
- E-017 Flag after an impulse bar: A large impulse bar followed by small bars that hold its far half means the impulse side is not being unwound; a close beyond the impulse bar's extreme resumes the move as the pause traders give up.
- E-018 Swing-structure break: After a higher swing low (lower swing high), a close through the last swing high (low) completes an intraday trend structure that swing traders watch; their entries and the stops of the counter side push it on.
- E-019 Floor-pivot touch rejection: Floor-trader pivots (P, R1/S1, R2/S2 from yesterday's H/L/C) are plotted by most retail platforms, so take-profit and fresh limit orders rest there; a bar that reaches a pivot from one side and closes back on that side shows the resting orders absorbed the push, so trade away from the level.
- E-020 Range-exhaustion extreme fade: Once today's range already equals a large share of the 20-session average daily range, day traders' targets are met and fresh momentum buyers are scarce; a new session extreme made then is more likely to be the profit-takers' exit than a new leg, so fade it.
- E-021 Trend-filtered RSI(2) dip: Connors-style: inside a trend defined by a long intraday EMA, a 2-period RSI extreme against the trend is short-horizon liquidity selling (or buying) by impatient traders, which trend holders absorb; buy the dip above the EMA, sell the pop below it.
- E-022 Heavy-volume balance break: A window that trades much more volume than usual for its time of day while the price stays inside a normal-width range means a large participant is accumulating against passive liquidity; when the range finally breaks, the side that was absorbing has won, so follow the break.
- E-023 Half-hour mark momentum: Execution algorithms and discretionary desks re-evaluate on clock marks; at each :00/:30 bar close, a trailing move larger than typical for its horizon means a parent order is still being worked into the next slice, so follow its sign.
- E-024 High-volume bar level hold: The midpoint of the most abnormal-volume bar of the last hour or two is where a large participant transacted; when price returns to it and closes back on its side, that participant defends the price (adds or covers), so trade the bounce away from the level.
- E-025 Consecutive-close run fade: A run of N consecutive same-direction closes that also covers more ground than usual is driven by short-term chasers and market-order bursts that exhaust the near book; liquidity providers then lean against it, so fade the bar that completes the run.
- E-026 Busy-session range break: A session whose cumulative volume is well above normal for the minute is an information day: informed flow is persistent, so a close beyond the recent high/low on such a day is followed by more of the same flow; follow the break only on busy days.
- E-027 Day-range retracement hold: After a day leg larger than usual, a pullback to a fixed share (38/50/62%) of the day's range is where missed participants and many retail Fibonacci users place their entries; a bar that reaches it and closes back in the leg's direction resumes the leg.
- E-028 Quiet-session range fade: On a session with cumulative volume well below normal for the minute there is no informed flow; a close beyond the recent high/low is noise pushed by small orders into thin books and mean-reverts as market makers re-center, so fade the break only on quiet days.
- E-029 Parabolic SAR flip follow: The Parabolic SAR is a trailing stop plotted by most retail platforms; when price trades through it the trend-followers using it are stopped and reverse at the same price, and their combined stop-and-reverse orders push the first bars of the new direction, so follow the flip.
- E-030 Prior-session value band re-entry: Yesterday's volume-weighted mean +/- k volume-weighted sd is the price zone the market accepted (Market Profile value area); a close back inside it from outside means the excursion was rejected by responsive traders, who push price back toward yesterday's value centre, so fade toward it.
- E-031 Signed-volume imbalance follow: When most of the recent volume traded on bars closing in one direction and the price moved that way, order flow is one-sided; order flow is persistent because large parents are split over time, so the imbalance crossing a high share predicts more of the same flow.
- E-032 RSI divergence at a new extreme: A new recent high made with a 14-period RSI well below its recent peak means the push is weaker than the previous one: fewer aggressive buyers are left and the late entrants are the first to exit, so fade the new extreme (mirror for lows).
- E-033 Second-entry pullback (H2/L2): In an EMA-defined trend, the first attempt to resume after a pullback (H1) often fails as counter-trend traders sell it; when the second attempt (H2) breaks the prior bar's high, the counter-trend side has failed twice and covers while trend traders re-enter, so follow it.
- E-034 Multi-horizon momentum alignment: Traders on different horizons (minutes, quarter-hours, hours) act on their own trend; the bar where the short, middle and long trailing returns first all agree is when every horizon's flow points the same way, leaving no natural seller, so follow the alignment.
- E-035 Acceptance near the session extreme: When price spends a while just under the session high without pulling back (accepted, not rejected), sellers there are being absorbed and stops build above; the close through the high then releases them, so follow the break only after such acceptance.
- E-036 Anchored-VWAP reclaim from the session extreme: The VWAP anchored at today's high is the average cost of everyone who traded since the top; a close back above it after a while puts that whole cohort of sellers under water, so they cover and buyers regain control (mirror at the low); follow the reclaim.
- E-037 Narrow-day extreme break follow: After the first hour, a session whose range is still small next to the 20-session average has not met day traders' range expectations; range is mean-reverting across days, so a close through the session extreme on such a day starts the expected expansion; follow it.
- E-038 Rotational-market edge fade: When price keeps crossing the midpoint of its recent range many times per hour, the market is in balance with liquidity providers on both edges; a close near (not beyond) an edge meets their resting orders and rotates back, so fade the edge touch only in rotational conditions.
- E-039 Fair-value-gap retest hold: A three-bar gap (bar low above the high two bars earlier) marks a burst of one-sided market orders that left no two-sided trade in the zone; when price first comes back into the zone and closes back out on the impulse side, the impulse side's remaining resting orders absorbed the late counter-traders; follow it.
- E-040 Clock-hour open defence: Hourly candles are the frame many desks and chart traders read; after the hour ran well away from its open, a return to the hour's open that closes back on the excursion side shows the side ahead for the hour defending a green (red) candle, and the retest-sellers are trapped; trade back toward the excursion.
- E-041 Pre-flatten day-move fade: In the last hour or two the day traders who rode the day's move (and retail accounts facing broker auto-flatten) must close, which is order flow against the day's direction; on a day already moved more than usual for the minute, a bar closing beyond the previous bar against the move starts that unwind; fade.
- E-042 Weak-volume new-extreme push fade: A new recent high made on clearly less volume than the previous push to a high, and closing in the lower half of its bar, means fewer aggressive buyers answer each new price while sellers stay; the push is the last buyers' exhaustion, so fade it (mirror for lows).
- E-043 Accelerating-burst exhaustion fade: Consecutive same-direction bars with each range larger than the last, ending in an unusually large bar, are a stop/momentum-ignition cascade; once the next bar fails to extend and closes against it, the forced orders are spent and liquidity providers pull price back; fade.
- E-044 Failed strong-close bar fade: A large bar that closes at its extreme attracts chasers at the close; when the very next bar closes back beyond that bar's midpoint, the chasers are under water at once and their stops sit just behind, so their exits push the reversal; fade the strong bar.
- E-045 Wick-imbalance range break: When the recent bars carry much longer lower wicks than upper wicks, dips keep being bought by passive buyers while offers are lifted without rejection; a close through the window's high then confirms the absorbing side has won; follow the break in the direction of the wick imbalance.
- E-046 ADR-fraction level fade from the open: Day traders set targets as fractions of the average daily range measured from the session open; at each open +/- j x frac x ADR take-profit orders rest, so a bar that reaches such a level from the open side and closes back shows the targets absorbing the push; fade back toward the open.
- E-047 Three-push wedge fade: Three separate pushes to new short-term highs inside one window (a wedge) show each leg being sold into again; when the bar after the third push closes below the prior bar's low, the late buyers of the third push are trapped and the move reverts; fade (mirror for lows).
- E-048 Prior-session closing VWAP rejection: The volume-weighted price of yesterday's closing window is where index funds and institutions executed their close-benchmarked orders; they defend or re-enter near it, so a bar that reaches it from one side and closes back on that side meets their resting orders; trade away from the level.
- E-049 Volume ignition after a dry-up: After a stretch where volume runs well below its normal level for the time of day, nobody informed is trading; the first bar with volume several times normal is new information arriving (not churn), and the passive side quoting the quiet market is run over, so follow the ignition bar's direction.
- E-050 Setup 9.1 failure stop run: Brazilian retail day traders widely run Larry Williams' setup 9.1 on WIN: buy above the high of the bar that turns the 9 EMA up, stop below that bar's low. When the setup triggers and price then closes back below the turn bar's low, those clustered retail stops are hit together and the trapped longs' exits push the move on; follow the failure (mirror for the sell setup).
- E-051 Wide outside-bar fade: A wide bar that takes out both the previous bar's high and low has swept the stops on both sides; when it closes at one extreme, that last sweep was forced stop and chase orders rather than new interest, and with both stop pools spent liquidity providers lean against it; fade the close side.
- E-052 Developing POC migration: The developing point of control (the price bin with the most volume today) is where the session has accepted value; when it jumps to a higher bin, buyers have moved the bulk of trade up and responsive sellers failed to hold value lower, so initiative flow continues that way; follow the shift.
- E-053 Hourly opening-range break: Execution desks and algos restart schedules at each clock hour, and the first minutes of the hour set a local range many short-term traders mark; the first close beyond it within the hour shows which side the new hour's flow is on, so follow the first break of each hour.
- E-054 Exact-tick repeated level break: Several recent bars topping at exactly the same tick show a refreshed resting order (an iceberg) defending it; once a bar closes beyond it the absorber is filled or gone, the stops behind the level fire and the traders who leaned on it cover; follow the break.
- E-056 Clock-mark bar impulse fade: At the :00/:15/:30 marks scheduled orders (time-sliced algos, timed stops, alerts) hit the book together, so a mark bar that is large for its minute and closes decisively is a mechanical burst, not new information; once it is done liquidity returns and price reverts; fade the mark bar.
- E-057 Decelerating run reversal: A run of same-direction bars whose bodies shrink bar after bar shows each push finding fewer aggressive takers; when the next bar closes against the run, the late chasers of the weakest push are under water and the momentum side has stopped paying up; fade the run.
- E-058 ADX trend-start follow: Wilder's ADX is on most retail platforms and a cross above its threshold is a common 'trend has started' trigger; when it crosses, directional movement has become one-sided and DI-based trend followers join in the leading DI's direction, extending the move; follow the cross.
- E-059 OBV leads the price high: When session on-balance volume makes a new high for the window while price is still below its window high, volume on up bars is outpacing volume on down bars before the price has moved (patient accumulation); the sellers capping the price are being worked through, so price follows the volume line (mirror for lows).
- E-060 Low-volume pullback resumption: In a session trend (price on the rising side of a rising VWAP), a pullback of several bars each on below-normal volume for its minute shows no real supply, only profit-taking (a Wyckoff 'test'); the first close above the previous bar's high is the trend holders resuming, so follow it (mirror in a down trend).
- E-061 Session-range traverse follow: Crossing from the bottom to the top part of the day's developed range within a short time is initiative activity by a longer-timeframe participant, not responsive rotation; such a participant keeps working the order, so the traverse continues past the far edge; follow it (mirror for a fast fall).
- E-062 Rising-volume run follow: Consecutive higher closes each traded on more volume than the bar before show participation building into the move: more and more traders are paying up rather than a single burst; flow that keeps growing is a parent order being scaled up, so follow the bar that completes the run when its volume is at least normal.
- E-063 Impulse-bar midpoint hold: A large near-full-body bar is a burst of one-sided market orders; its midpoint is the average fill of that burst. The first return to the midpoint that closes back on the impulse side shows the impulse side defending its average price while the counter-traders who faded it are trapped; follow the impulse.
- E-064 Hikkake inside-bar fakeout: After an inside bar, a break of only one side of it draws breakout traders in that direction; if price then closes beyond the OTHER side of the inside bar within a few bars, those traders are trapped with stops just behind and the release of the compression goes the other way; follow the fakeout reversal.
- E-065 Open-at-extreme bar follow: A bar of at least typical range whose open is its low (no trade below the opening price for the whole bar) and that closes in its upper half shows buyers lifting offers from the first tick with no seller able to push below; demand that strong at the bar's start is a parent order still running, so follow it (mirror).
- E-066 TD setup count exhaustion fade: DeMark's TD setup (N consecutive closes above the close four bars earlier) is plotted by many platforms and marks a move carried by late chasers; when the count completes, the trend followers are fully in and counters on many screens flag exhaustion at the same bar, so fade it (mirror for a down count).
- E-067 Stochastic pop with the VWAP side: Bernstein-style 'stochastic pop': the fast stochastic reaching overbought is strength, not exhaustion, when price is on the buying side of the session VWAP; short-horizon momentum traders enter there and the oscillator faders' stops sit just above, so follow the pop (mirror for oversold below VWAP).
- E-068 Up-on-light, down-on-heavy volume asymmetry fade: When price has risen over the window but the average down bar trades much more volume than the average up bar, the rise is drifting on thin buying while sellers distribute size on every dip; once the asymmetry becomes large the distributing side controls the price, so fade the rise (mirror for a fall).
- E-069 Bar-open gap fill fade: A bar that opens well away from the previous bar's last trade and keeps the gap at its close was printed through an empty book (liquidity providers pulled quotes for a moment), not by new information; once quotes refill, the vacuum is closed by passive traders leaning back toward the last trade, so fade the gap.
- E-070 Aroon crossover follow: Chande's Aroon measures how recently the window's high and low were made and is a stock trend trigger on retail platforms; when the oscillator swings to a strong positive (negative) value, the latest extreme is fresh while the opposite one is stale, and the timing-based trend followers who use it enter together, extending the move; follow the cross.
- E-071 Volume-clock bucket move follow: In volume time (buckets of a fixed number of contracts, Easley/Lopez de Prado), a bucket whose price change is large means the contracts in it were traded mostly by one side; informed parents are split across buckets, so the next bucket keeps the sign; follow a large completed bucket.
- E-072 PFR reversal-close bar (Stormer): The PFR (preco de fechamento de reversao) is one of the most traded Brazilian retail setups: a bar that makes a low below the two previous lows (and the recent low) but closes above the previous close shows the sellers' push failing inside one bar; the sellers who sold the new low are trapped and the many setup users buy right after it, so follow the reversal (mirror for highs).
- E-073 Hidden RSI divergence continuation: On the buying side of the VWAP, a higher price low made while the RSI makes a lower low means the pullback was sharp but did not cost the trend any ground: momentum-based sellers were absorbed by trend holders; the first close above the prior bar's high resumes the trend (mirror below the VWAP).
- E-074 VWAP skew in the day's range: When the session VWAP sits well above the midpoint of the day's range, most of today's contracts traded in the upper part: buyers own the day's inventory and lower prices were rejected quickly; short-term breaks up in such a session are carried by that inventory owner, so follow a short-term high break only when the volume skew agrees (mirror).
- E-075 Unfilled opening-gap direction break: An overnight gap that has not been filled at all during the session is overnight information that local traders failed to reverse; the gap side holds the inventory, so intraday breaks in the gap direction are followed while breaks against it are not traded; follow N-minute highs (lows) on unfilled gap-up (down) days.
- E-076 Session-VWAP slope turn: The session VWAP is the average cost of everyone who traded today; when its slope turns clearly positive with price above it, the average participant is being forced to pay up and late shorts are losing on the day's average, so VWAP-benchmarked execution and covering keep lifting price; follow the slope turn (mirror).
- E-077 Daily-candle colour flip follow: The session open is the zero line of every day trader's daily candle and of intraday P&L for the day; after price has stayed on one side of it for a while, a close back across flips the daily candle's colour on every chart, triggering day traders' bias changes and the stops of those positioned for the old colour; follow the flip.
- E-078 Prior-session close-location carry break: A session that closed near its high means buyers were still adding into the close (late informed or rebalancing flow not yet finished); that flow carries into the next session, so today's short-term high breaks are followed after a strong close and low breaks after a weak close (mirror).
- E-079 VWAP first-touch hold: After price has traded entirely on one side of the session VWAP for a while, VWAP-benchmarked buyers (sellers) who missed the move rest orders at the day's average cost; the first bar that touches VWAP and closes back on the old side shows them absorbing the pullback sellers, so the move resumes away from VWAP (the hold, not the cross of E-001).
- E-080 VWMA-SMA volume confirmation cross: Dormeier's VPC: when the volume-weighted mean of recent closes rises clearly above the plain mean, the heavy-volume bars are the higher-priced ones, i.e. size is paying up rather than drifting; with price above the mean, the side committing volume is the side of the trend, so follow the cross (mirror).
- E-081 Heikin-Ashi colour flip after a run: Heikin-Ashi candles are a default chart type for many retail WIN traders, who hold while the candles stay one colour and reverse on the first opposite-colour candle; after a run of red HA candles the first green one triggers their covering and new longs together, and the shorts' stops sit just above, so follow it.
- E-082 Agulhada do Didi: Didi Aguiar's 'needle' (3, 8 and 20-bar averages all crossed by one candle, then fanning out in order) is one of the most watched Brazilian retail setups; it marks the end of a compression, and the many users buy when the next candle breaks the needle candle's high in the order 3>8>20, so follow the fan-out (mirror).
- E-083 Narrow-range streak release: Several consecutive bars with a range well below normal for their minute mean liquidity providers are quoting a balanced book with no initiative flow; the first bar of at least normal range that closes strongly in one direction is the first initiative order to clear that book, and the quotes pulled by the burst let it travel, so follow the release bar.
- E-084 Larry Williams setup 9.2 trigger: Setup 9.2 (rising 9 EMA, a bar closing below the previous low, buy on the break of that bar's high) is taught to most Brazilian WIN day traders; their buy-stops cluster one tick above the pullback bar, so the trigger break fills them together while the pullback sellers' stops are at the same place; follow it (mirror).
- E-085 Untested prior-session level magnet: Stops and breakout orders accumulate just beyond yesterday's high, low, close, open and midpoint while they stay untouched today; when price comes within a typical 15-minute move of such a level, short-term traders run it toward that liquidity before it is touched, so follow the approach toward the level.
- E-087 Session path-efficiency trend follow: The share of today's total price path that is net progress from the open measures how one-sided the day is; when it rises well above its usual value for the minute, the counter side is not rotating price back, which means a persistent informed or rebalancing flow, so follow the direction when efficiency jumps.
- E-088 Setup 123 local-bottom trigger: The '123' (middle of three candles makes the lowest low, here also the lowest of the window, entry on the break of the third candle's high) is a Stormer-popularised Brazilian setup; the clustered buy-stops of its users fill together at the trigger, and the sellers of the local low are trapped with stops above, so follow the trigger (mirror for tops).
- E-099 Round-number rejection: Retail and discretionary WIN traders anchor take-profits, limit entries and option strikes on round index levels (multiples of 250/500/1000 points), so resting liquidity clusters there; the first bar that reaches the level from below and closes back under it shows that liquidity absorbing the push, so fade away from it.
- E-100 Prior-session POC rejection: Yesterday's point of control (the price bin with the most volume) is where the largest share of yesterday's inventory was built; those holders defend their cost, so the first touch of it from one side that closes back on that side meets their orders and price moves away from the level.
- E-101 Morning/evening star after a run: A large bar making the lowest close of a run, followed by a small indecision bar and then a bar closing back above the large bar's body midpoint, shows the run's last burst found no follow-through and buyers retook half its ground; the late sellers of the burst are trapped, so follow the three-bar reversal (mirror).
- E-102 Choppiness-index trend onset: Dreiss' Choppiness Index compares the summed true range to the net high-low span; after a choppy stretch (above 61.8) a fall below a low threshold means the bars have started to line up in one direction, i.e. the balance broke and one side is now initiating; follow the direction of the window's move.
- E-103 Late-session intraday momentum (first-window sign): Gao, Han, Li and Zhou: the market's first half-hour return from the prior close predicts the last half-hour, because institutional rebalancing and informed orders delayed to the close trade the same way; in the closing window, enter in the sign of the opening return whenever price is on that side of a short EMA.
- E-104 Volume-climax stall fade: At the end of a run, the bar with the highest volume of the window (and far above normal for its minute) is the buying climax where the last chasers and stops are filled; when the next bar trades much less, fails to extend and closes below the climax close, demand is spent, so fade the run (mirror for a selling climax).
- E-105 Raschke Anti stochastic hook: Raschke's 'Anti': the slow %D sets the momentum trend, the fast %K pulls back toward it and then hooks back in the trend direction; the hook marks the end of the counter-trend correction, when trend traders re-enter and the correction traders cover, so follow the hook (mirror).
- E-106 Hoffman inventory retracement bar: In a steep EMA trend, a bar whose open and close sit at least 45% below its high is counter-trend inventory being worked by short-term sellers while institutions pause; a close above that bar's high within three bars shows the counter-trend selling is done and the trend side resumes, so follow it (mirror).
- E-107 Previous clock-hour extreme rejection: Hourly candles are what most desks and chart traders read, so the completed previous hour's high and low hold resting orders and take-profits; the first bar of the session that pokes through such a level and closes back inside meets that liquidity and the poke's buyers are trapped, so fade back into the hour's range.
- E-108 Williams squat-bar break: Bill Williams' squat bar (volume up while range per contract falls) is a standoff where heavy two-sided trade makes no progress; whichever side then closes beyond the squat bar's range within a few bars has absorbed the other, whose stops sit just beyond it, so follow the break.
- E-109 Realized-skewness reversal: A window of 1-bar returns with strong positive skew means the rise came from a few large up bars (chasers and stop bursts) while most bars drifted down; like lottery-like assets, the spiky side is overpaid and liquidity providers lean against it, so the first down bar while skew is high starts the reversal (mirror).
- E-110 AB=CD measured-move completion fade: Swing traders project the second leg equal to the first (AB=CD) and park take-profits and harmonic counter-entries at D; when a zigzag leg after a 38-89% pullback reaches D, that resting liquidity absorbs the push and the leg's late buyers are the exit, so fade at D (mirror).
- E-111 Mass Index reversal bulge: Dorsey's Mass Index sums the EMA ratio of bar ranges; a bulge above ~27 that falls back means a range expansion has run its course, which tends to end the prevailing swing; trade against the 9-EMA slope at the moment the bulge completes.
- E-112 Negative Volume Index cross follow: Fosback's NVI accumulates price change only on bars where volume falls, the bars traded by patient participants rather than the crowd; when that quiet-bar price path breaks clearly away from its own EMA, the patient side is moving price without noise traders, so follow it.
- E-113 Converging-triangle break follow: Flat highs with rising lows (ascending triangle) show a fixed seller being met by buyers raising their bids every dip; once that seller is filled and price closes above the flat top, the stops above and the impatient bidders lift it, so follow the break (mirror for a descending triangle).
- E-114 Calm-dollar WIN extreme fade: Foreign and macro flow into Brazil moves the dollar and the index together; when WDO has barely moved in the last 30 minutes, a WIN close beyond its recent high/low is local flow without macro backing, which market makers fade back into the range.
- E-116 Impulsive-vs-corrective body asymmetry follow: When up bars have much larger bodies than down bars over a window, rises are made by initiative market orders while dips are mere drift (corrective); the impulsive side controls the tape, so follow it when the asymmetry appears with price above its window mean (mirror).
- E-117 Slow-pullback resumption: After a fast impulse leg, a pullback that travels much slower than the impulse is passive profit-taking, not new supply; the first bar closing above the previous bar's high shows the impulse side returning before the pullback gains speed, so follow it (mirror).
- E-118 Close-vs-midpoint divergence fade: When closes rise over a window but bar midpoints do not, the gains are printed in the last seconds of each bar (closing pushes) while most trading stays where it was; the push is cosmetic and reverts once the pushers stop, so fade it (mirror).
- E-115 WIN-led dollar catch-up: Equity risk news reaches the index future first (S&P arbitrage, foreign equity desks); FX dealers re-price the dollar a little later, so a sharp WIN move not yet mirrored by WDO predicts the dollar moving the opposite way; trade WDO against the WIN jump.
- E-119 Higher-timeframe trend, EMA reclaim entry: Trade only in the direction of the long moving average of hourly closes (the post: 100 EMA on 1H; here the 50/100-hour simple mean); enter when price closes back across a short EMA in that direction.
- E-120 VWAP wick rejection on heavy volume: Not the first touch: a bar that pierces the session VWAP, leaves a long wick and closes back on its side with volume well above normal for the minute shows larger players defending the level; trade the bounce.
- E-121 First-hour trend lock: The direction of the first 30-60 minutes sets the day; afterwards only trade in that direction, entering when price closes back across a short EMA.
- E-122 Broken parabolic (engulfing after a run): After several bars in a row of one colour, the first engulfing bar the other way marks exhaustion; fade it.
- E-123 RSI exhaustion reversal: RSI at an extreme (80-90+ or below 10-20) stretches the rubber band; the first bar that closes against the move starts the snap back.
- E-124 Volume spike without movement, follow the drift: A bar with very high volume but a small range during a slow grind is a large player filling orders (refill); trade with the grind's direction.
- E-125 Opening-range rip and dip: In the first two hours, a break of the opening range, a one-bar dip, then a close back above the dip bar's high continues the break (mirror for shorts).
- E-126 Flag fake breakdown: After an impulse, a flag forms; the first poke beyond the flag against the impulse that closes back inside traps the breakout sellers and the real move follows the impulse.
- E-127 Opening reversal (the '9:45' reversal at B3's open): Early-session moves overshoot; 15-45 minutes after the open, fade a move from the open larger than usual for that time of day.
- E-128 Noise-area break from the open: Zarattini/Aziz/Barbon: moves from the open (or yesterday's close, whichever is farther) that stay inside the average absolute move for that minute are noise traded by liquidity providers; a close outside that band means a real supply/demand imbalance (informed or rebalancing flow) that keeps working in the same direction, so follow the first close outside the noise area.
- E-129 Ichimoku cloud break with Tenkan/Kijun alignment: The Ichimoku cloud is a default overlay for many retail traders, who treat a close out of the cloud with Tenkan above Kijun as a trend start; their entries and the stops of those leaning on the cloud edge fire together at the break, so follow the first close outside the cloud when the two lines agree (mirror).
- E-130 Multi-session price stretch fade: When the price sits far above its own 20-session mean (in 20-session standard deviations), swing holders are deep in profit and new multi-day buyers are scarce; the first intraday bar that closes below the previous bar's low starts their profit-taking, so fade the stretch on that counter bar (mirror).
- E-132 DiNapoli double repo reversal: After a thrust of consecutive closes above the 3x3 displaced average, a close below it, a close back above and a second close below within a few bars shows the thrust buyers failing twice to resume; the late buyers of the bounce are trapped and DiNapoli users sell together, so follow the reversal (mirror).
- E-133 Opening-gap fade in the first hours: A gap larger than usual is set by overnight orders placed at the open without two-sided local liquidity; once the opening orders are filled, local day traders and market makers lean back toward yesterday's close, so in the first part of the session sell each bar that closes below the previous low while price is still above yesterday's close on a gap up (mirror).
- E-136 Inside-yesterday balance edge fade: While today's whole range stays inside yesterday's (after the first part of the session), the market accepts yesterday's value and nobody initiates; responsive sellers work the top part of yesterday's range and buyers the bottom, so a counter bar in the outer part of yesterday's range starts a rotation back toward its middle (mirror).
- E-137 Midday balance break: From 12:00 the market trades in a low-liquidity midday balance while local desks are at lunch; when they and the US afternoon flow return, the first close outside that balance shows which side the afternoon initiative is on, and the stops resting just beyond the tight midday range push it on; follow it.
- E-138 Choppy-window micro-break fade: When Kaufman's efficiency ratio over the last 30-60 minutes is low, price is churning between liquidity providers on both sides and nobody initiates; a close beyond the previous three bars' high/low in such a window is a small order pushing through a thin quote, which the market makers fade, so fade it.
- E-139 Regression-channel dip in a straight trend: When the closes of the last hour sit on a straight line (high R-squared), one participant is working a steady parent order; a close more than k residual deviations against that line is a short-term liquidity seller (buyer) the parent order absorbs, so buy the dip below a rising line (mirror).
- E-140 Initial-balance extension level fade: Market Profile traders project multiples of the initial-balance width (first 30/60 minutes) beyond the IB high/low and scale out there; a bar reaching such an extension from the inside and closing back short of it shows those take-profits absorbing the push, so fade back toward the IB.
- E-142 Prior-hour close retest hold: A completed hourly candle that closed strongly (large body for its range) leaves its close as the price where the hour's winners marked their position; a return to that close that holds on the hour's side meets their adds and the late counter-traders are trapped, so trade in the hour's direction.
- E-143 Vacuum-bar fade: A bar with a range far above normal for its minute but only normal-or-less volume moved through an empty book (quotes pulled for a moment), not on new information; when quotes refill, liquidity providers lean the price back, so fade the bar's direction.
- E-145 Channel-midline rotation follow: After price tags the edge of its recent channel and then closes back through the channel midline, the edge buyers are under water and the rotation traders target the opposite edge; follow the midline cross toward the other side.
- E-146 Swing-leg stretch fade: A zigzag swing leg (reversal threshold r) whose length is far above the typical leg of the last 20 sessions has outrun the swing traders' usual targets; once a bar closes against the leg, their profit-taking and the late entrants' exits revert it, so fade the stretched leg.
- E-147 Quiet-drift fade: Several consecutive closes in one direction on bars much narrower than normal for their minute is quote shading by liquidity providers in an empty market, not initiative buying; with no committed side behind it the drift reverts, so fade the bar that completes the drift.
- E-148 Deep-book micro-break fade: When the last hour's price impact (range per contract traded) is well below its 20-session norm, the book is deep and liquidity providers absorb small pushes; a close beyond the last 5 minutes' high/low in such a regime is a small order moving through refilled quotes and reverts, so fade it.
- E-149 Double-sided sweep, follow the second break: A break of the recent high soon followed by a close below the recent low has run the stops on both sides; the first-side breakout buyers are now trapped and liquidating together with the fresh breakdown sellers, so the second break carries; follow it (mirror).
- E-150 Volatility-decay retracement after a storm: A 30-minute burst of abnormal activity with a directional move, followed by a quiet stretch at half the storm's pace, means the forced flow is done and the market makers who took the other side now hold inventory they lean back on; once the calm sets in, trade against the storm's move.
- E-151 Close-to-close vs range variance ratio trend follow: When close-to-close variance rises above the Parkinson range variance of the same bars, bars are closing near their extremes instead of rotating inside them: a one-sided order is lifting each bar's close; the crossing of that ratio marks persistent flow, so follow the window's direction.
- E-152 Yesterday's closing-hour push reversal: A last hour that moved much more than usual is closing-benchmark and rebalancing flow, not information; the inventory dealers took on at the close is unwound in the next morning, so in the first part of today's session trade counter bars against yesterday's closing-hour push.
- E-153 Previous clock-hour overreaction fade: A completed clock hour that moved far more than a typical hour attracted hourly-chart chasers at its close; in the first minutes of the next hour the first bar closing beyond the previous bar against that hour shows the chasers are not being followed, and their exits revert the overreaction; fade the hour.
- E-154 Momentum re-acceleration follow: In a move larger than usual for its horizon, a slowdown of the short-term speed WITHOUT a pullback that is followed by a renewed speed-up shows the parent order resumed after a pause rather than meeting supply; a re-accelerating move still has an active initiator, so follow it (mirror).
- E-155 Leverage-effect asymmetry fade: In an equity index, volatility normally rises when price falls and fades when it rises; an up move with rising volatility is a short squeeze of forced buyers, and a down move with falling volatility is orderly selling with no fear behind it; both lack the usual participants, so fade them.
- E-156 Post-wide-day consolidation fade: After a session whose range was much wider than the 20-session average, day traders cut size and dealers rebuild inventory; the next session tends to consolidate inside the shock, so short-term breaks of the recent high/low are not followed and are faded on that next day only.
- E-157 Bollinger-inside-Keltner squeeze release: When the close-based standard deviation band sits inside the range-based Keltner channel, closes are pinned while bars still trade (two-sided absorption); the first bar where the bands expand back out is when one side stops absorbing, and the many squeeze-indicator users enter with the momentum; follow the side of the close relative to the mean.
- E-158 Risk-incoherent index move fade: WIN and WDO normally move against each other because foreign flow buys the index and sells the dollar together; when both rise (or fall) clearly over the same window, the index move has no foreign flow behind it, only local traders, and the dealers who absorbed it lean it back; fade the index move.
- E-159 Session-midpoint first-touch hold: On a day with a wide range, the midpoint of the session's high-low is the line day traders use to call the day bullish or bearish; after price has traded only in the upper half for a while, the first touch of the midpoint that closes back above meets the buyers defending the day's bias; trade away from it.
- E-160 Hour-end painting fade: When the last minutes of a clock hour print the hour's high and the hour closes on it, short-term traders pushed the hourly candle's close (cosmetic marking for hourly charts and alerts) rather than new information; once the new hour starts the pushers stop and the price gives the mark back; fade it.
- E-161 Participation turn follow: When the volume of the last window crosses well above its normal level for that time of day while price has moved, new participants are entering on one side (an information or rebalancing event starting), and that flow is split over the following bars; follow the window's direction at the turn.
- E-162 Wide indecision bar break follow: A bar much wider than normal that closes in its middle third ran the stops on both sides and left both camps trapped near its extremes; the first close beyond its high (low) within a few bars shows which side gave up, and its exits plus the new entries push the price on; follow the break.
- E-163 Volatile-dollar index break follow: When the dollar is trading a range far above normal, macro and foreign desks are repositioning across Brazilian assets; index breaks of the recent high/low in that regime carry macro order flow rather than local noise, so they extend; follow WIN breaks only while the dollar is volatile.
- E-164 Macro-backed dip buy: A falling dollar over the last hour signals foreign money coming into Brazil, which keeps bidding for the index; a short-term index dip to a new 15-minute low in that regime is local selling the foreign flow absorbs, so the first bar closing above the previous bar's high is bought (mirror with a rising dollar).
- E-165 Round-trip rejection follow: When price runs far from where it stood a window ago and then closes back below that starting price, the whole excursion was rejected: everyone who bought the run is under water and the responsive sellers have proven the higher prices unacceptable, so the return keeps going; follow the completed round trip.
- E-166 Dollar-high rejection buys the index: A new short-term dollar high that is knocked back sharply in the next bar shows FX dealers selling the dollar spike (foreign inflow or central-bank-sensitive supply); the index, priced off the same foreign flow, moves the other way with a lag, so buy WIN on the dollar rejection (mirror at a dollar low).
- E-167 Fizzled day-move follow-through: A move from the open that reached well beyond the usual distance for the time of day and has now fallen back near the open within the hour has failed: the trend-day buyers are trapped above and their exits push price further back; follow the fizzle in the direction of the retreat.
- E-168 Post-settlement drift fade: WIN's daily settlement price is set from the 17:00-17:15 window; after it, the cash market is closed and index arbitrage is gone, so a futures-only drift away from the settlement-window average is thin speculative flow that dealers lean against (they hedge at tomorrow's cash open); fade a stretch from that average on the first bar closing back toward it.
- E-169 Indecision cluster at an extreme, break against: Several normal-size small-body bars sitting at the high of the last hour show buyers and sellers trading two-way at the top with no buyer paying up; when price then closes below the cluster's low, the buyers who stood in the cluster are trapped and the push is over, so sell (mirror at the low).
- E-170 Dollar-priced index break follow: Foreign investors, the marginal Ibovespa buyers, watch the index in dollars (WIN / WDO); a new window high of that ratio while WIN in reais is still under its own window high means foreign holders already see a breakout and their stops/entries trigger, which then lifts WIN; follow it (mirror).
- E-171 Heavy-churn session edge fade: A session trading far more contracts than usual for the minute while its range is no wider than usual is dominated by two-sided size (hedgers, arbitrage, market makers) absorbing both directions; in that regime a bar that pokes to a new short-term extreme but closes against its open meets that absorption, so fade it.
- E-172 Failed VWAP-band reversion follow: A stretch from the session VWAP draws mean-reversion traders; when price reverts toward VWAP and then stretches out beyond the band again, the faders of the first stretch are trapped a second time and their stops plus the trend side's adds carry the move; follow the second exit from the band (mirror).
- E-173 Failed-breakout high re-break follow: A poke above the recent high that closes back inside invites fade traders who put their stops just above the poke's high; a later close above that failure high within the next minutes runs those stops and shows the breakout side has absorbed the faders, so follow it (mirror at lows).
- E-174 Alternating-tape end follow: A run of closes that flip sign every bar is quote bouncing by liquidity providers with no initiator; two same-sign closes in a row that also clear the alternating zone's high mark the first initiative order breaking the bounce, and makers pull quotes in its direction; follow it (mirror).
- E-175 Overnight dollar-gap index catch-down: An opening dollar gap well above normal is overnight risk-off news priced by FX dealers; when WIN opened without pricing at least half of it, foreign equity desks sell the index during the morning to catch up, so sell WIN counter bars (close below the prior low) early in the session (mirror for a dollar gap down).
- E-176 London-close move fade: European desks that trade Brazilian exposure flatten into the London close (12:30 or 13:30 in Sao Paulo, by season); a WIN hour that moved much more than usual into that time carried their closing flow, which then stops, so in the following hour fade the move on the first bar closing back past the prior bar.
- E-177 Absorbed heavy counter bar in a session trend: In a session trend (price on the rising side of a rising VWAP), a counter bar on heavy volume is a large seller hitting bids; when the very next bar closes above that bar's high, the trend side absorbed the whole sale and the seller is under water at once, so the trend resumes; follow it (mirror).
- E-178 Liquidity-clock break fade: When the seasonal bar range for this minute (20-session median) is clearly below its level 30 minutes earlier, the session is entering a quieter part of its daily liquidity clock (participants leaving); a close beyond the recent high/low then has no arriving flow behind it and is faded by liquidity providers.
- E-179 Separation-bar follow in a sticky tape: When consecutive bars have overlapped heavily for a while, makers quote a two-sided book; a bar trading entirely beyond the previous bar (no overlap at all) means an initiator cleared every resting quote at once, and makers widen and step away in that direction, so follow the separation.
- E-180 Morning-value afternoon reversion: The VWAP of the morning (before 12:00) is the price where the cash-session participants built the day's inventory; an afternoon stretch far from it is carried by the thinner afternoon crowd, and the first counter bar starts the return toward the morning value as morning holders defend and afternoon chasers exit.
- E-181 Elder impulse colour flip follow: Elder's Impulse System colours a bar green when both the 13-EMA and the MACD histogram rise and red when both fall, and its many users act on the colour; the first green bar after a red one means both inertia and momentum turned together, the red-bar sellers are stopped and impulse users buy at once, so follow it.
- E-182 VWAP-agreement balance micro-break fade: When the volume-weighted price of the last window sits on top of the session VWAP, recent trade is happening at the day's average cost: the market is in balance around value and no one is migrating it; a close beyond the last 15 minutes' high/low in that state is a small push that responsive traders fade.
- E-183 Yesterday-range step expansion follow: Day traders gauge today's progress in units of yesterday's range; each time today's range crosses a further fixed fraction of it on a new session extreme, the stops of those who sized for a day like yesterday sit just beyond and expansion traders add, so follow the bar that makes the new step.
- E-184 Elder force-index dip in an EMA trend: Elder's force index (price change x volume, short EMA) going negative inside a rising trend shows a pullback with sellers committing volume; when it turns back positive while price still holds above the trend EMA, the volume-backed selling is done and trend holders resume, so buy (mirror in a down trend).
- E-185 Woodies CCI zero-line reject: In Woodies CCI, a CCI that was recently above +100 and pulls back toward but not through the zero line, then hooks up, marks a shallow correction inside an intact push; the CCI traders buy the hook together and the faders' stops sit just above, so follow the hook (mirror below zero).
- E-186 Close-pin release follow: Several bars in a row closing at almost the same price while still trading a range mean a large limit order is pinning that price and absorbing both sides; a bar that then closes a full typical range away shows the pin was filled or pulled, and the side it was holding back runs, so follow the release bar.
- E-187 Ehlers Fisher transform extreme turn fade: The Fisher transform of the price's position in its recent range turns the bounded position into a near-Gaussian score that makes extremes sharp; when it turns down from a high extreme, the short-term buyers who pushed price to the top of its window stopped paying up and the many Fisher users sell the turn, so fade (mirror from a low extreme).
- E-188 Decoupled-tape micro-break fade: WIN and WDO normally move against each other because foreign flow trades both; when the rolling correlation of their 1-bar returns is no longer clearly negative, the index tape is local churn without macro flow, so a close beyond the last 10 minutes' high/low is a small local push that liquidity providers lean back; fade it.
- E-189 PTAX-window index spill fade: During the four 10-minute PTAX consultation windows (10:00, 11:00, 12:00, 13:00) dealers push the dollar for the fixing and foreign desks hedge index exposure mechanically, so a WIN move made inside the window is fixing-related flow, not information; once the window closes that flow stops, so fade the window move.
- E-190 Change-of-character counter bar follow: After a move larger than usual for the window, a counter bar whose body is larger than ANY trend-direction body of the window shows the counter side now trades with more urgency than the trend side ever did (Wyckoff change of character); the trend holders are no longer the strongest flow, so follow the counter bar.
- E-191 Two-close volume acceptance break follow: Market Profile traders treat a single close beyond a range as a probe and two consecutive closes beyond it as acceptance; when both closes also trade at least normal volume for their minute, the new prices are being accepted by real participation rather than a stop sweep, so responsive traders give up and the move extends.
- E-192 Chandelier-stop sweep fade: Many trend followers trail stops at the chandelier level (recent high minus m x ATR); a close through it fills that whole cluster of forced sell orders at once, and once the stop flow is spent nobody is left to sell, so liquidity providers who absorbed it lean the price back; buy the sweep (mirror at the short stop).
- E-193 Volume-clock milestone momentum: Execution algorithms schedule parent orders against the expected daily volume curve and check progress at volume milestones; when the session crosses each fixed share of a typical day's volume while the day has already moved more than usual from the open, the scheduled remainder of those orders pushes the same way.
- E-194 Dollar-jump index overreaction fade: When the dollar jumps in one bar, index algorithms re-price WIN off it mechanically and overshoot; a WIN bar moving the opposite way much more than usual for its minute in the same bar is that mechanical repricing, and once the dollar news is absorbed local liquidity providers lean it back; fade the WIN reaction bar.
- E-195 MFI failure swing: Wilder-style failure swing on the volume-weighted Money Flow Index: after money flow was overbought, a bounce that fails below the level and then breaks the prior MFI reaction low shows buying volume drying up on each attempt; the volume-backed buyers have left and the price follows the money flow down (mirror for oversold).
- E-196 Decaying-volatility micro-break fade: When the recent bar ranges have shrunk well below those of the window before, the active flow of the earlier window is finished and market makers are re-centering their quotes; a close beyond the last 15 minutes' high/low in that state is a leftover push with no initiator behind it, so fade it.
- E-197 Balanced-flow micro-break fade: When volume on up bars and down bars over the window nearly cancel, aggressive buyers and sellers are trading against each other with no net side; a close beyond the last 15 minutes' high/low in that balance is not backed by net flow and is reabsorbed by the other side, so fade it.
- E-208 Wick-ceiling closing-high fade: A new N-minute closing high that still sits below the window's intrabar high means closes are climbing back into a zone where sellers already rejected prices with a wick; those sellers' resting offers are still above and the buyers chasing the closing high meet them, so fade the closing-basis break (mirror for lows).
- E-209 Post-narrow-session break follow: Crabel's narrow-range idea across sessions: after a session whose range was well below the 20-session average, volatility is compressed and range expansion is due; day traders sized for a quiet day keep tight stops just beyond short-term extremes, so today's breaks of the recent high/low extend; follow them only on the day after a narrow session.
- E-210 Mid-range micro-break fade: On a day with a normal-or-wide range, a break of the last few minutes' high/low that happens in the middle of the session range is not at any level anyone watches: no stops or breakout orders sit there, only a small push through quotes that liquidity providers lean back on; fade breaks made mid-range.
- E-211 Seasonal reversal-regime micro-break fade: Some times of day are dominated by liquidity provision (bar returns reverse), others by order-flow continuation; the lag-1 autocorrelation of bar returns at this minute over the last 20 sessions measures which; where it is negative, a close beyond the recent high/low is quote bouncing that market makers reverse, so fade it only in the reversal-prone times of day.
- E-212 Overhead-supply break-even fade: When a large share of the last hours' volume traded above the current price, many of those buyers are under water; a rally to a new 15-minute high brings price back toward their cost and they sell to get out at break-even, capping the push; fade the break into heavy overhead volume (mirror: trapped shorts below).
- E-213 Developing daily-candle tail bias: After the first hour, a session whose high stands far above both its open and the current price is drawing a long upper tail on every daily chart: higher prices were tried and rejected, and day traders read the day as bearish; the first bar closing below the previous low joins that rejection (mirror for a long lower tail).
- E-214 Renko reversal after a brick run: Renko charts are widely used by WIN day traders; bricks sized from the typical daily range turn colour only after a two-brick move against the run. After a run of several bricks the run's holders exit on the first reversal brick and Renko users reverse at the same time, so follow the reversal brick.
- E-215 Two-sided-session micro-break fade: When, after the opening half hour, the session has extended both its high and its low within the recent window, both stop pools were swept and the day is rotating around value with responsive traders on both sides; a short-term break that does not reach a new session extreme is then reabsorbed, so fade it.
- E-216 Calming-tape momentum follow: Volatility-managed momentum: a move larger than usual for its horizon made while bar-to-bar volatility is falling is orderly, persistent flow (a parent order being worked), not a forced burst that reverts; follow the move at the bar it becomes large while the tape is calming.
- E-217 Open-drive day pullback resumption: A session whose open is still near its low an hour or more in (and price above VWAP) is an open-drive day: initiative buyers lifted from the first trade and nobody could push below the open. A short pullback of one to three lower closes is profit-taking, and the first close above the previous high is the drive resuming; follow it (mirror for an open near the high).
- E-218 Opening-auction price hold: The session's first trade is set by the opening auction, where the overnight orders were matched; desks that missed it rest orders back at that price. After price has traded entirely on one side of the open for a while, the first bar that touches it and closes back on the old side shows those orders absorbing the pullback traders, so the move resumes away from the open (a hold, not the colour flip of E-077).
- E-219 Creep-break fade: A close beyond the recent high/low made by a bar much narrower than normal for its minute is not an initiative order sweeping the book, just quotes inching past the level; with no aggressive side and the resting liquidity of the range still in place, liquidity providers lean it back inside, so fade it.
- E-220 Counter-value micro-break fade: A short-term break upward made while price is clearly below the session VWAP pushes into prices the day's average participant already sold at; the holders of the day's inventory (short below value) add there and the break buyers are a small local push, so fade breaks made against the value side (mirror).
- E-221 Session break-failure memory fade: Each session has its own character, set by who is trading it: when most of today's earlier short-term breaks closed back through the broken level within ten minutes, today's book is run by responsive liquidity providers and the breakout traders keep losing; the next break is faded on that evidence alone.
- E-222 Spent-hour micro-break fade: When the last 60 minutes already covered far more range than a typical hour, the hour's directional flow and the short-term traders' range expectations are used up; a further short-term break then meets profit-takers and no fresh initiators, so fade it.
- E-223 Prior-settlement pin micro-break fade: Yesterday's close/settlement is the zero line of variation margin and of many option and hedge books; dealers hedging near it buy dips and sell rallies, which pins price around it, so short-term breaks made while price sits close to yesterday's close are faded.
- E-224 New-extreme balance dip buy: Counting today's bars that set a new session high against those that set a new session low measures who has been initiating all day; when the count is lopsided toward highs, the dips are profit-taking against a persistent buyer, and the first close above the previous high after a lower close resumes the day (mirror).
- E-225 Multi-day channel edge micro-break fade: Swing traders rest take-profits and fresh counter orders at the high/low of the last 5-20 sessions; a short-term intraday break reaching that multi-day edge meets their size rather than the thin intraday book, so fade breaks made at or near the multi-session channel edge (mirror at the low).
- E-226 Steady-participation move follow: TWAP/POV execution algorithms slice a parent order into evenly sized trades, so a window with unusually uniform per-bar volume (low coefficient of variation) and a larger-than-usual move is the footprint of a scheduled order still being worked, not a burst; follow the move when that steady regime appears.
- E-227 Fixed-point target cluster fade: WIN day traders commonly use fixed point targets (150/200/300 points) from their entry near a recent swing low; when price has risen that many points from the window's low, their take-profit sells cluster there and the late buyers meet them, so fade the bar where the run first reaches the distance (mirror).
- E-228 Dollar non-confirmation (SMT) extreme fade: Foreign flow buys the index and sells the dollar together, so a real WIN breakout to a new window high comes with WDO near its own window low; when WIN breaks out while WDO still sits in the upper part of its range, the break is local flow the macro side does not confirm, and dealers lean it back; fade (mirror).
- E-229 Inside-higher-timeframe-candle micro-break fade: A short-term break that still closes inside the previous completed 15/30/60-minute candle is no break at all for the traders reading that higher timeframe: their resting orders and stops sit at that candle's high/low, not here, so the small breakout buyers get no follow-through and the move reverts; fade it.
- E-230 Day-after-balanced-session micro-break fade: A session that closed almost where it opened (small body for its range) ended in balance: both sides accepted the range and nobody carried conviction overnight; the next session's participants trade responsively, so its short-term breaks lack an initiator and are faded.
- E-231 Heavy-volume ceiling break fade: When the recent window's high was printed by a bar trading well above normal volume for its minute, a large seller showed size at that price; a later close just above it runs into that same participant's remaining offers rather than an empty book, so the break is absorbed and reverts (mirror at lows).
- E-232 No-overnight-news day micro-break fade: A session opening almost at yesterday's close (gap small next to the 20-session typical gap) brought no overnight information to be priced, so no informed parent order is being worked during the day; intraday breaks are then local noise that liquidity providers fade.
- E-233 Late MACD signal-cross fade: A MACD signal-line cross in the direction the MACD already sits (bullish cross above zero, bearish below) is a late, crowded retail trigger arriving after the move; the short-term traders who buy it are the liquidity the earlier movers exit into, so fade the cross.
- E-234 Front-loaded-session micro-break fade: When the first hour took a larger share of the session's volume than usual for the time, the day's information was traded early and the rest of the session is digestion by responsive traders; later short-term breaks have no new initiator behind them, so fade them.
- E-235 Off-peak-minute micro-break fade: At minutes whose seasonal volume (20-session median) sits well below the session average, the participants who carry information (institutional desks, foreign flow) are mostly absent; breaks then are small orders through quotes that market makers fade, regardless of whether liquidity is rising or falling.
- E-236 Jump-free tape micro-break fade: When bipower variation explains all of the recent realized variance, the price moved by many small steps and no jump (no news, no forced burst): a diffusive, maker-dominated tape; a 10-minute break in that regime is one more random step that liquidity providers lean back on, so fade it.
- E-237 Early-extreme side micro-break fade: The day's high or low is often set early; when the first hour made its high before its low, the early high is the extreme the morning sellers defend and the day's bias is down, so after the first hour short-term breaks upward toward it are faded (mirror when the low came first).
- E-238 Calm-week micro-break fade: When the last 5 sessions' average range is well below the 20-session average, the multi-day volatility regime is calm: dealers carry small inventories, no macro repositioning is under way and day traders cut their range expectations; a close beyond the recent high/low in such a week is a small push through quotes that liquidity providers lean back on, so fade it.
- E-239 Previous-block midpoint rejection: The 50% level of the last completed 15/30/60-minute candle is where chart traders reading that timeframe split the candle into buyers' and sellers' halves and rest their re-entries; a bar that comes back to it from one side and closes back on that side shows those orders absorbing the pullback, so trade away from the midpoint (only when the block was wide enough to matter).
- E-240 Stair-step break exhaustion fade: Several closes above the short-term high within half an hour with no opposite break is a stair-step of small breakout entries; each step adds late buyers with stops just below, and once the count is high the breakout pool is used up and their stops sit close, so fade the bar where the count is reached (mirror).
- E-241 Wicked break-bar fade: A bar that closes above the recent high but leaves a large upper wick traded well beyond the level and was already sold back inside the same bar: sellers met the breakout at the new prices and the buyers who bought the bar's high are under water at once; fade the break (mirror for lows).
- E-242 Multi-day overlap balance micro-break fade: When yesterday's range overlapped most of the day before's, the market has accepted the same price zone for two sessions (multi-day balance): swing traders sell its top and buy its bottom and nobody initiates; today's short-term breaks inside that regime lack an initiator and are faded.
- E-243 Gap-filled day micro-break fade: Once an opening gap of meaningful size has been fully filled back to yesterday's close, local traders have reversed the overnight information and the session is run by responsive liquidity providers rather than by an overnight initiator; from then on short-term breaks are faded.
- E-244 Outside-block micro-break fade: When the current clock block (30/60/120 minutes) has already traded beyond both the high and the low of the previous block, the stop pools on both sides of that candle are spent and the block is rotating two-way; a further short-term break inside such an outside block has no fresh stops to run and is faded.
- E-245 VWAP-contested session micro-break fade: Many closes crossing the session VWAP over the last two hours mean neither side can hold price away from the day's average cost: value is contested and both camps trade responsively around it; a short-term break in that state is absorbed by the other camp, so fade it.
- E-246 Clock-hour range quota fade: Short-term traders size targets on what a clock hour usually covers; when the current hour's range first reaches its 20-session typical range for that hour, the bar that set the new hour extreme is where their take-profits sit and the hour's directional flow is spent, so fade that bar.
- E-247 Developing doji-day micro-break fade: After the first 90 minutes, a session whose price is back near its open despite a real range is drawing a doji on every daily chart: both sides tried and were rejected, and the day is being traded responsively; short-term breaks in that state lack an initiator and are faded.
- E-248 Prior-sessions opening-price poke fade: The opening-auction prices of the last few sessions are where each day's overnight orders were matched, and desks that anchor on them keep resting orders there; a bar that trades through such a price and closes back meets those resting orders, so the small break buyers (sellers) are trapped; fade back from the level.
- E-249 Multi-session rolling VWAP hold: The volume-weighted price of the last few sessions plus today is the average cost of the swing holders; when price returns to it from one side and closes back on that side, those holders defend their cost basis (add or cover) instead of the intraday traders who pushed into it; trade away from the level.
- E-250 Multi-day range quartile poke fade: Swing traders grid their orders on the quarter, half and three-quarter marks of the last few sessions' range; a bar that pokes through one of those marks and closes back has met that resting size, and the intraday push through it has no follow-through; fade it.
- E-251 Prior-sessions initial-balance edge fade: The first-hour high/low of recent sessions is the range that the opening institutions defended on those days, and Market Profile users carry it forward as reference; a later poke through such an old edge that closes back is absorbed by the same responsive traders, so fade it.
- E-252 Vortex cross fade: The Vortex indicator cross is a stock retail trend trigger that fires after the short swing already happened; the traders who enter on it supply the liquidity the earlier movers exit into, so the swing reverts; fade the cross.
- E-253 Prior sessions' peak-volume price hold: The price of the heaviest-volume bar of each recent session (outside the auctions) is where the largest participant of that day transacted; that participant defends or re-enters there, so a return to the price that closes back on its side is held; trade away from the level.
- E-254 Daily moving-average poke fade: The 5/9/20-day moving averages of daily closes are plotted on almost every WIN daily chart, and swing traders rest entries and exits on them; an intraday poke through the daily average that closes back meets that resting size, not an empty book, so fade it.
- E-255 Prior-session swing-point poke fade: Yesterday's intraday swing highs and lows are the levels chart traders mark overnight, with stops and fresh orders just around them; a poke through one that closes back means the stops were taken and the resting counter orders won, so the late breakout traders are trapped; fade it.
- E-256 Prior closing-range break follow: The high/low of yesterday's last half hour to hour bounds the price where the overnight positions were opened, and those holders' stops sit just beyond it; a close through the closing range forces them out and their exits extend the move, so follow the break.
- E-257 Prior settlement-close hold: Each recent session's close is the zero line of variation margin for positions opened that day; holders defend it to avoid paying margin, so a return to such a close that ends the bar back on its side is held; trade away from the level.
- E-258 Hourly candle close-location carry: Desks and chart traders read hourly candles; an hour that was wider than usual and closed in its top (bottom) slice ends with buyers (sellers) still lifting at the bell, i.e. a parent order not finished. The faders who sold the hour's high are offside as the next hour opens, so follow the close side.
- E-259 Low-volume micro-break fade: A close through the recent high/low printed on far less volume than usual for that minute was not pushed by informed size, only by a few small orders into a thin book; the liquidity providers who were run over re-quote and lean back, so fade the break.
- E-260 Pre-New-York lull micro-break fade: Between the settled B3 open and the New York cash open, large index participants wait for US price discovery; breaks of the recent high/low in this window are local noise traded against market makers who are happy to provide liquidity, so they revert. Fade breaks only in the 9:30-10:25 lull.
- E-261 Marginal-break fade: A close that clears the recent high/low by only a sliver triggers the breakout orders and the stops resting just beyond, but no buyer is paying up past them; once that small pool is filled there is nobody left on the break side and the resting liquidity pushes price back, so fade small breaks.
- E-262 Stacked daily-extreme micro-break fade: Each of the last few sessions' highs and lows is a level where swing traders keep take-profit and fresh limit orders; a 15-minute break that runs into ANY of them (not only yesterday's or the channel edge) meets that resting supply/demand and stalls, so fade breaks that end near a stacked old extreme.
- E-263 Indecisive-tape micro-break fade: When the recent bars are mostly small bodies inside their ranges, every bar is a two-way fight that the passive side wins; a break of the 15-minute extreme in such a tape is one more probe that the same passive traders absorb, so fade it.
- E-264 Counter-drift micro-break fade: After a two-hour drift larger than usual, a 15-minute break against the drift is short covering or a dip-buyer's probe, while the participant behind the drift is still working the order; that order leans on the counter break, so fade it back toward the drift.
- E-265 Spent volume-quota micro-break fade: Once the session has already traded most of a typical full day's volume, the day's big orders have been executed; later breaks of the recent high/low are not backed by fresh size and the participants who are done (and now take profit) meet them, so fade breaks after the volume quota is spent.
- E-266 Flat-value micro-break fade: A session VWAP that has barely moved for half an hour means volume keeps trading at the same value on both sides: neither buyers nor sellers are moving value; a break of the recent high/low then trades away from accepted value and responsive traders return it, so fade breaks when value is flat.
- E-267 Range-ahead-of-schedule micro-break fade: When today's range is already much wider than the usual range at this minute, day traders' targets are met early and the move is ahead of its normal schedule; new breaks then mostly carry late chasers who meet profit-takers, so fade breaks of the recent high/low on such days.
- E-268 Tweezer extreme fade: Two consecutive bars that stop at exactly the same high (low) at the top (bottom) of the recent window show one resting limit order capping two separate pushes; when the second bar closes down in its lower half, the buyers who pushed into it are trapped under an offer that did not move, so fade (mirror at lows).
- E-269 Scheduled-calm-ahead micro-break fade: The intraday volatility schedule is very stable across sessions; when the range usually traded over the NEXT minutes at this time of day is well below the range of the minutes just gone (the active window is ending, e.g. after the open or before lunch), no fresh initiators are due, so a 15-minute break made then has nobody to extend it and liquidity providers lean it back; fade it.
- E-270 Dollar-unbacked VWAP stretch turn fade: Foreign flow lifts the index and sells the dollar together, so a WIN stretch far above its session VWAP is backed when WDO trades below its own session average; when WIN is stretched above value while WDO still sits above its average, the stretch is local flow, and the first bar closing below the previous low starts the unwind back to value; fade it (mirror below VWAP).
