- E-271 VSA no-demand / no-supply bar fade: Volume Spread Analysis: an up bar at the top of the recent window on a narrower range than the previous bar and less volume than either of the two previous bars shows professional money is not buying the rise; the push is carried by the last small buyers only, so sell it (no-supply bar at the low: buy).
- E-272 Counter-VWAP-slope new-extreme fade: The session VWAP's slope over the window shows where volume-weighted value is migrating; a new window high while value is migrating down means price is probing up on little volume while the volume keeps trading lower; the day's value-setters sell the probe, so fade it (mirror for a new low while value rises).
- E-273 Scheduled-volatility-ramp break follow: When the range usually traded over the next minutes at this time of day is much larger than the range just gone (the NYSE open, scheduled US data, the European close), informed participants are about to arrive on schedule; a 15-minute break made as the ramp starts is the first of their orders and the liquidity providers widen instead of leaning, so follow the break.
- E-274 Candle-open stop-run fade: Chart traders reset stops and bracket orders at the open of each new 15/30/60-minute candle just beyond the last minutes' extreme; a break printed on the very first bar of a new candle is that one-off burst of reset stops and brackets being triggered, not an initiator, and once it is filled the price reverts; fade breaks made on a candle's first bar.
- E-275 Seasonal break-outcome fade: Who dominates each half hour of the session (liquidity providers or breakout traders) repeats from day to day; when over the last 20 sessions the 15-minute breaks made in the trailing half hour of this time of day were, on average, given back within the next minutes, today's break at the same time meets the same providers, so fade it.
- E-276 Spike-made ceiling break fade: When most of the window's range was made by one bar, its high (low) is the tip of a single burst that was never accepted: the burst ran into resting orders there and price left at once; a later close through that tip meets the same untouched orders and no accepted value behind it, so fade the break.
- E-277 Dollar-at-value micro-break fade: When WDO trades near its own session average price, the dollar is in balance and foreign desks are not repositioning across both markets; with the macro side idle, a short-term WIN break is local flow that index liquidity providers absorb, so fade it (a level-of-the-dollar regime, not its volatility).
- E-278 Dense-profile micro-break fade: A close beyond the recent high/low that lands in a price zone where a large share of today's earlier volume already traded is breaking into accepted inventory: the holders from that zone (and the passive orders that made it a high-volume node) sell into the up-break and buy the down-break, so the small breaker is faded.
- E-279 Round-number cross follow (stop cascade): Stop-loss buy orders cluster just above round numbers and stop-loss sells just below; a close through a round level triggers that cluster, and the executed stops are market orders that push the price further (Osler's price cascades), so follow the close through the round number.
- E-280 High-low spread-regime micro-break fade: When the Corwin-Schultz high-low spread estimate over the last window runs well above its 20-session norm, bar ranges are made mostly of bid-ask bouncing between liquidity providers rather than of information; a 10-minute high/low break then is the bounce reaching the far quote, which the providers lean back, so fade.
- E-281 Expanding-range run follow: Consecutive same-colour bars whose ranges grow each bar, ending with a bar of at least normal range for the minute, show an aggressive parent order accelerating while market makers widen and pull quotes; the urgency is not spent at the bar close, so follow the run's direction.
- E-282 Intraday head-and-shoulders neckline break follow: Three swing highs with a higher middle and roughly level shoulders mean the last push failed below the head; chart traders who see the pattern sell the close through the neckline and the longs from the right shoulder have stops just under it, so the neckline break is followed (inverse pattern for longs).
- E-283 Multi-session-trend micro-break follow: When yesterday closed above the average price of the last several sessions, swing holders are long and positioned to add; intraday breaks in that direction are joined by their adds and by short covering, while breaks against it meet their dip-buying, so follow only breaks aligned with the multi-session trend.
- E-284 Half-gap level hold: On a meaningful opening gap, gap-fade traders take profit at the half-gap (midpoint of yesterday's close and today's open) and gap-continuation traders buy it; a bar that reaches the half-gap and closes back on the gap side shows that interest absorbing the fill, so trade back toward the open.
- E-285 Camarilla H3/L3 fade, H4/L4 break: Camarilla levels (yesterday's close +/- 1.1 x range / 4 and / 2) are a published day-trader map: users sell the first touch of H3 that closes back below and buy L3, but chase a close beyond H4/L4; their shared orders make those reactions self-fulfilling at the level.
- E-286 Heavy-opening-auction day micro-break fade: A session whose opening bar trades much more than its usual volume priced the overnight information in the auction itself; the rest of the day has no informed parent order left to work and is run by responsive liquidity providers, so after the first half hour short-term breaks are faded.
- E-287 Three-line-break reversal follow: On a three-line-break chart a reversal needs a close beyond the extreme of the last N same-colour lines, i.e. the whole recent leg is undone on closing prices; the leg's late entrants are then under water and exit while the chart's users enter the new direction, so follow the reversal after a leg of at least N lines.
- E-288 Intraday trendline touch hold: A rising line through the session's last two higher swing lows (falling line through two lower swing highs) is drawn by chart traders who rest buy (sell) orders on it; the first touch that closes back on the trend side shows those orders absorbing the pullback, so trade the hold; a close through the line voids it.
- E-289 Williams VIX-fix fear/greed spike reversal: Larry Williams' synthetic VIX (drop of the low from the window's highest close) spiking far above its 20-session norm marks forced, fearful selling; once the spike stops growing and a bar closes above the previous bar's high, the panic sellers are done and liquidity providers who bought from them lift the price (mirror: a greed spike from the lowest close).
- E-290 Time-symmetric correction completion: After a leg larger than usual, a pullback that has lasted about half the leg's duration and retraced 20-80% of it without breaking down is a routine correction where the leg's holders did not exit; the first close beyond the previous bar's extreme at that time-symmetric point signals the correctors are done, so follow the leg.
- E-291 Keltner-band walk exhaustion: Several consecutive closes outside an EMA +/- ATR Keltner band are a one-sided chase by late momentum traders; the first close back inside the band shows the chase has run out of new buyers (sellers) and their positions unwind toward the mean, so fade the re-entry.
- E-292 VWAP-hug release follow: When every close of a window stays within a small distance of the session VWAP, benchmark-tracking execution dominates and no side has an edge; a close far beyond that hug band means a participant stopped tracking the benchmark and is demanding liquidity, and the VWAP traders who were pinned there re-hedge in the same direction; follow the release.
- E-293 Order-block retest hold: The last opposite-colour bar before an impulse bar is where the impulsive participant started filling; when price first returns to that bar's range and closes back beyond it in the impulse direction, the unfilled remainder of that order defends the zone, so trade with the impulse; a close through the zone or a long wait voids it.
- E-294 Heavy-volume takeover bar: Two consecutive bars both on abnormal volume for their minute, of opposite colour, where the second trades even more and closes beyond the first bar's open: the second side met the first side's heavy flow head-on and won; the losers of that contest exit over the next bars, so follow the second bar.
- E-295 Higher-timeframe structure low test: When the last two completed 30-90 minute blocks of the session made a higher high and a higher low, swing traders reading that larger chart buy at the last block's low; a 1-5 minute bar that tests that low and closes up on the right side is their support holding, so trade with the larger structure (mirror for lower highs).
- E-296 Close-location churn fade: Several consecutive bars that all close near their highs while the price makes no net progress mean buyers lift every bar into its close but a larger passive seller sells each new bar back down; the aggressive side is spending effort for no result and gives up, so fade it (mirror for closes near the lows).
- E-297 Fractal break while the Alligator sleeps: Bill Williams' Alligator lines (smoothed medians 13/8/5, shifted) bunched together mean the market is asleep and stops cluster just beyond the last confirmed fractal high/low; the first close through a fractal while the lines are still tangled is the start of a new swing that wakes trend traders, so follow it only out of the sleeping state.
- E-298 Broadening-window micro-break fade: When the last block made both a higher high and a lower low than the block before it, the market is a megaphone: two-sided stop runs by players who push each edge to trigger the other side and then reverse; a further short-term break in that state is one more head-fake that the responsive side sells (buys), so fade it.
- E-299 Weaker-than-the-level break fade: The bar that set the recent high was where the seller who capped it traded; a later close through that high on less volume than that bar means the breakers are smaller than the participant who defended the level, so his remaining size and the breakout buyers' stops reverse the break (mirror at the low).
- E-300 Stale micro-level break follow: A window high made early in the window and not approached in the recent part of it has had time to collect the stops of shorts and the buy-stops of breakout traders; the first close through such a stale level releases that pile at once and the market orders carry the price on, unlike a level made a few bars ago.
- E-301 Light-participation day micro-break fade: When the session's cumulative volume is well below its usual level for the time of day, the large institutional parent orders are absent and the book is run by local liquidity providers; short-term breaks then carry no initiator behind them and are leaned back, so fade them on light days.
- E-302 Flattening-hour micro-break fade: In the last hour or two before the close, day traders who must be flat stop opening positions and only unwind, and position takers have done their day; short-term breaks then are bursts of liquidation into a book that refills, not new information, so fade them.
- E-303 Day-after-trend-day micro-break fade: A session that closed at its high or low was a trend day (Raschke, Crabel); the next session usually digests it in a range while the trend-day holders take profit and late entrants rebalance, so its short-term breaks are faded.
- E-304 High bar-overlap micro-break fade: When consecutive bars overlap far more than usual, every bar trades back through the previous one: the auction is rotating two-way with liquidity providers on both sides; a close beyond the window extreme in such a tape is one more rotation reaching an edge, so fade it.
- E-305 Range-completion-schedule late extreme fade: The share of the day's final range already traded by each minute is stable across sessions; a new session high/low made at a time when, on recent days, the range was normally almost complete is an overshoot by late chasers that the day's done participants sell (buy) into, so fade it.
- E-306 Long-detached-from-VWAP micro-break fade: After price has traded entirely on one side of the session VWAP for a long time, everyone who traded at value is in profit and benchmark desks are behind; a new short-term break further away is where those holders take profit and VWAP-benchmarked orders lean against it, so fade the break (not the VWAP cross of E-001).
- E-307 Window volume-centre lag break fade: If the window's volume-weighted price sits below its plain average price, most contracts changed hands at the low side of the window: the up-moves came on little volume; an up-break of such a window is a light probe away from where the size traded and returns to it, so fade it (mirror for down-breaks).
- E-308 Flat-channel regression stretch fade: When the least-squares line through the last window's closes is nearly flat (its total rise is small next to the residual scatter), the market is a horizontal channel worked by liquidity providers; a close that stretches k residual sds off the line is a passing order the providers absorb, so fade back toward the line (no trend required, unlike the trend-dip of E-139).
- E-309 Wide-box micro-break fade: If the last window's high-low box is already much wider than the same window usually is at this minute, the move inside it has spent the day traders' range; a further close beyond the box edge is a late chaser in an overextended window that the responsive side leans against, so fade it (window width regime, not the session-range schedule of E-267).
- E-310 Bounce-regime big-bar fade: When today's recent one-bar returns are clearly negatively autocorrelated (each move is partly undone on the next bar), price changes are dominated by inventory-managing liquidity providers (Roll's bid-ask bounce at a larger scale); a bar of unusually large range in that regime is a transient push they unwind, so fade its direction.
- E-311 Harami after a wide bar fade: A wide bar followed at once by a small bar inside it means the wide bar's aggressor got no follow-through: the next participants traded only within its range and the chasers who entered at its close are not being rewarded; their exits push price back, so fade the wide bar's direction (the opposite of following an inside-bar break, E-005).
- E-312 Prior-close order-imbalance inventory fade: A one-sided volume imbalance in the last 30 minutes of yesterday (volume on up bars against down bars) left market makers holding the opposite inventory overnight; they unload it during today's first hours as liquidity traders arrive, so rallies after a buy-imbalance close are sold and dips after a sell-imbalance close are bought: fade early micro-breaks in that direction (signed volume, not yesterday's price push of E-152).
- E-313 Red bars, rising lows: Several consecutive bars that close below their open yet each make a higher low show sellers hitting the bid into every bar's close while a passive buyer keeps raising the floor; the aggressive sellers are being absorbed without progress and the absorber wins, so buy (mirror: green bars with lower highs, sell).
- E-314 Flat-day open-magnet stretch fade: When the price was still near the session open a short while ago (the day has not chosen a side), a fast move to an unusually large distance from the open for this minute is a burst, not a trend: the open stays the day's reference for the two-sided participants, who fade it back toward the open.
- E-315 Point-and-figure double-top break follow: Point-and-figure chartists (Dorsey) buy when an X column rises above the previous X column's top and sell when an O column falls below the previous O column's bottom; their simultaneous orders and the stops of the counter side resting at those columns push the first boxes of the break, so follow it (one signal per column; boxes as a fraction of the session open).
- E-316 Stale-tape micro-break fade: A high share of bars that close exactly at the previous close (Lesmond's zero-return measure) means the price is pinned by thick resting orders and few informed trades arrive; a 15-minute break in such a stale tape is a small order poking through a refilling book, so fade it.
- E-317 Clock-hour anchored VWAP stretch fade: Execution algorithms restart their schedules at each clock hour and benchmark the hour's VWAP; a close far outside that hour's volume-weighted band (k volume-weighted sds) is an impatient order ahead of the schedule, and the hour's benchmark traders fill against it, pulling price back to the hour's value (an hourly anchor, not the session VWAP of P-016).
- E-318 Record-pullback dip buy on a trend day: On a day already moved more than usual from the open, a pullback that first becomes deeper than every earlier pullback of the session triggers the trailing stops of the day's holders all at once; that forced selling is absorbed by the participant still driving the day, so buy the record pullback (mirror on a down day).
- E-319 Dollar-at-extreme climax index break fade: A WIN break to a new short-term low while WDO sits at its own session high means both legs of the risk-off trade are stretched at once: the foreign desks' hedges are already in, the last sellers are the late locals, and macro dealers stop adding at the dollar extreme; fade the WIN break (mirror).
- E-320 Yesterday's same-minute price poke fade: Day traders compare today's tape with where the market stood at the same time yesterday (overlay charts, intraday P&L versus a day ago); after price has stayed on one side of yesterday's same-minute price, a bar that pokes through it and closes back meets those anchored orders, so trade away from it.
- E-321 Falling-participation micro-break fade: When the volume of the last window is clearly below the volume of the window before it today, the participants who were trading are leaving; a 15-minute break in that fading tape has no new size behind it and liquidity providers lean it back, so fade it (today's own volume decay, not the seasonal clock).
- E-322 Widest-bar extreme poke fade: Today's widest bar is the session's biggest one-shot burst; its high and low are where that burst met the resting orders that stopped it. A later bar that pokes through that extreme and closes back meets the same untouched liquidity, so the poke buyers (sellers) are trapped; trade away from the level.
- E-323 Yesterday's VWAP cross follow: Yesterday's full-session VWAP is the average cost of yesterday's inventory; after price has stayed on one side of it for a while, a close across puts that whole cohort from in-profit to under water (or the reverse), and their exits plus swing traders' entries push price on; follow the cross.
- E-324 Volume-decoupled tape micro-break fade: Normally bigger price steps come with more volume (information moves price with size); when the recent correlation between absolute bar moves and bar volume is low, price steps are made by quote shifts, not by committed size, so a 10-minute break then is noise that liquidity providers fade.
- E-325 Dollar session-average flip, index follows: When the dollar, after trading on one side of its own session average price for a while, crosses back through it, the day's FX flow has turned (foreign money now leaving or entering); equity desks act on the same flow a little later, so trade WIN against the dollar's flip.
- E-326 Morning-range afternoon break fade: The 09:00-12:00 range is where the cash session's institutions did the day's main trade; an afternoon close beyond it is usually carried by the thinner afternoon crowd, while the morning participants hold their orders at their own range edges; fade the afternoon break back into the morning range.
- E-327 Contained impulse bar fade: A bar much wider than usual for its minute and almost all body that still stays inside the last 30 minutes' range hit the book hard without reaching any level where stops or breakout orders rest; with nothing to cascade, the aggressor's push is the whole move and market makers re-centre; fade it.
- E-328 Overnight tug-of-war micro-break fade: Lou/Polk/Skouras: overnight and intraday returns pull in opposite directions because individuals push prices overnight while institutions trade during the day against that flow. When the last few sessions' overnight returns sum clearly one way, intraday institutions lean against it, so short-term breaks in the overnight direction are faded (up-breaks sold after overnight gains, down-breaks bought after losses).
- E-329 Unextended initial-balance micro-break fade: Dalton: while the session has not yet extended beyond its initial balance (first 30-90 minutes), the opening institutions' range is holding and the day is still in balance, with responsive traders at both IB edges; short-term breaks inside the IB are small pushes they absorb, so fade them until the first range extension.
- E-330 Rushed-approach micro-break fade: A break of the window high reached by a traverse of the whole window from its low within a few bars is a burst of urgent market orders that emptied the book on the way up; the burst is the move and it arrives at the level spent, while the resting offers above remain, so the break reverts; fade it (mirror).
- E-331 Open-VWAP conflict micro-break fade: When price sits between the session open and a clearly separated session VWAP, the two most-watched intraday references give opposite verdicts: open-anchored day traders and VWAP-anchored desks take opposite sides, so neither camp follows a short-term break and the other absorbs it; fade breaks in that conflicted zone.
- E-332 Open-inside-prior-value micro-break fade: Dalton: an open inside yesterday's value area that stays accepted there signals a balance day in which yesterday's extremes usually hold and responsive traders dominate; while the session opened and still trades inside yesterday's volume-weighted value band, short-term breaks lack an initiator and are faded.
- E-333 Opposite-push conviction fade: Compare the size behind the two ends of the window: a close through the window high on a bar trading much less than the bar that set the window low means the down push had more conviction than the up break; the heavier participant is still on the other side and leans on the light breakers; fade (mirror).
- E-334 Seasonal deep-book minute micro-break fade: At times of day when volume per point of range (20-session same-minute medians) is high next to the session average, the book is deep: many contracts trade for little price change because liquidity providers quote size; a short-term break at those minutes pushes into refilled depth and reverts.
- E-335 Decelerating-approach break fade: If the window's closes rose but the latest third rose less than the one before (negative curvature), buying pressure is fading as price arrives at the high; the break is the last small push of a slowing move, the parent order behind the rise is finishing, and the late breakout buyers are the exit; fade (mirror for a decelerating fall).
- E-336 Thin-book session micro-break fade: When today's range per contract traded so far is far above its usual value at this minute, the session's moves are being made by small orders through a thin book rather than by size; such impact-driven moves overshoot and liquidity providers who re-quote pull them back, so short-term breaks on such sessions are faded.
- E-337 WIN price at the dollar's session extreme hold: The WIN price printed when WDO set its latest session high (low) marks the index level at the peak of the day's risk-off (risk-on) flow, where foreign desks finished hedging; when WIN later returns to it and closes back on its side, those desks defend the level instead of adding, so trade away from it.
- E-348 Frozen day-range micro-break fade: When neither the session high nor the session low has moved for a long time, the day's range is established and both edges are held by responsive traders; a short-term break inside that frozen range reaches no level with stops behind it and the small breaker is leaned back by liquidity providers, so fade it.
- E-349 Multi-day value micro-break fade: When the price sits near the mean price of the last 20 sessions (inside k multi-day sds), it is at the swing traders' accepted value: they sell above and buy below it and no multi-day initiator is pushing; short-term breaks there are local pushes the multi-day holders absorb, so fade them.
- E-350 Post-heavy-volume-session micro-break fade: A session that traded far more contracts than the 20-session norm was an event day whose large orders were executed then; the next session is digestion by dealers rebuilding inventory with no parent order left to work, so its short-term breaks are faded (yesterday's volume, not its range or close).
- E-351 Inside-noise-band micro-break fade: Moves from the open that stay inside the average absolute move for this minute (the Zarattini/Aziz noise area) are the territory of liquidity providers; while price is still inside that band, a break of the recent high/low is noise they lean back, so fade it (the complement regime of following an exit, E-128).
- E-352 VWAP-launch break follow: A short-term break made soon after the price touched the session VWAP, on the VWAP side it now trades, starts from the day's average cost: benchmark buyers who filled at value hold the inventory behind it and nobody is trapped above, so the push away from value carries; follow the break (not the touch-hold of E-079).
- E-353 Daily-reversal-regime micro-break fade: When the last 15 daily close-to-close returns are negatively autocorrelated, each day undoes the last: the multi-day market is run by mean-reverting swing traders rather than trend flow; in that regime intraday short-term breaks also lack follow-through and are faded (daily-scale autocorrelation, not intraday).
- E-354 Volume-drought week micro-break fade: When the last 5 sessions traded clearly fewer contracts than the 20-session norm, institutional participants are away (holidays, waiting for an event) and the book is run by local market makers; short-term breaks then have no size behind them and are leaned back, so fade them.
- E-355 Compact-value session micro-break fade: When today's volume-weighted dispersion of trade around the session VWAP is small next to its usual value at this minute, the day's volume is concentrated at one value that both sides accept; a short-term break away from that compact value is a probe the value traders return, so fade it.
- E-356 Previous-hour VWAP hold after value migration: When the last completed clock hour's VWAP stepped well above the hour before, value is migrating up and the last hour's buyers hold inventory at that VWAP; the first bar that dips to it and closes back above meets their defence, so buy (mirror for a downward step).
- E-357 Unfilled gap-zone micro-break fade: While price trades inside a meaningful overnight gap that has not been filled (between yesterday's close and today's open), it is in a zone with no inventory from either session, where gap-fill traders and gap holders both trade responsively; short-term breaks inside it are absorbed, so fade them.
- E-358 Counter-hour-candle micro-break fade: A short-term up-break printed while the current clock-hour candle is still clearly red (price well below the hour's open) is a small push against what hourly-chart desks see as a selling hour; the hour's sellers still hold the initiative and lean on it, so the breakout buyers get no follow-through: fade it (mirror for down-breaks in a green hour).
- E-359 Strong-hour pullback resumption: When the current clock hour has already moved far more than usual for this point of the hour, a parent order is being worked inside the hour; a one-bar counter close is profit-taking by short-term traders, and the next close beyond the previous bar's extreme shows the hour's initiator resuming, so follow it (mirror).
- E-360 Momentum-peak turn fade: When the trailing move, scaled by its usual size at this minute, peaks at the highest value of the last half hour and the next bar closes below the previous low, the urgency of the buyers has just topped out; the chasers who bought at peak speed are the first offside and their exits revert the move (mirror for a momentum trough).
- E-361 Twin-market same-direction break fade: Foreign flow buys the index and sells the dollar together, so a genuine WIN up-break comes with the dollar falling; when WIN breaks its recent high at the same time WDO is also breaking its own recent high, the two breaks contradict the macro flow and are local stop runs in both books that dealers lean back; fade the WIN break (mirror).
- E-362 Fast departure from VWAP snapback: A price that was sitting at the session VWAP and then ran a multiple of its usual move away within a few minutes was pushed by an impatient order, not by a migration of value; the VWAP-benchmarked desks fill against it, and once a bar closes back beyond the previous bar toward VWAP the burst is over, so trade back toward value.
- E-363 Counter-delta micro-break fade: The session's cumulative signed volume (bar volume signed by the bar's colour, over total volume) shows which side has been the aggressor all day; a short-term up-break while that cumulative delta is negative is a small push against the day's net seller, who keeps selling into it, so fade the break (mirror).
- E-364 Trend-day new extreme after a deep pullback: On a day already far from its open for the time of day, a pullback from the session extreme deeper than a typical 15-minute move shakes out the weak holders; when price then closes at a new session extreme anyway, the day's initiator absorbed the whole pullback and the shaken-out traders must buy back above, so follow the new extreme (mirror).
- E-365 Symmetric-volume day micro-break fade: When the session VWAP sits at the midpoint of the day's range, the day's volume is spread evenly across its range: neither buyers nor sellers own the inventory and both edges are defended responsively; short-term breaks in such a symmetric session have no inventory owner behind them and are faded.
- E-366 Inside-hour regime micro-break fade: When the last completed clock hour traded entirely inside the hour before it, hourly-chart traders see a contracting, two-way market with their stops parked at the bigger hour's edges; short-term breaks in the following hour happen inside that frame, reach no stop pool and are leaned back by liquidity providers, so fade them.
- E-367 Joint-calm index and dollar micro-break fade: When both WIN and WDO have traded a narrower last hour than usual for this time of day, neither local nor macro (foreign, FX) flow is active and the books are run by market makers in both markets; a short-term WIN break then has no informed side behind it and is faded.
- E-368 Low-energy break-bar fade: A close through the recent high/low printed by a bar whose range is small for its minute of day means the level gave way to a trickle of orders, not to an initiative burst; nobody with size is behind it, so liquidity providers lean on it and price returns into the box; fade the break.
- E-369 Value-interior micro-break fade: While the close is within k volume-weighted standard deviations of today's volume-weighted mean, price is still inside the session's accepted value, where responsive traders on both sides are active; a short-term break there is rotation inside value rather than initiative, so fade it.
- E-370 Clock-mark range-position fade on balanced days: At each :00/:30 mark desks and slicers reassess; on a day whose move from the open is not larger than usual for the minute, a close near the top (bottom) of the day's range at the mark is where the day's responsive sellers (buyers) rest, so lean against the range position toward the middle.
- E-371 Opening-price rotation regime micro-break fade: When price has crossed the session open several times in the last two hours, the day traders' zero line is being contested and neither side holds inventory; short-term breaks in that regime are pushed by stops and rotate back, so fade them only while the open keeps being crossed.
- E-372 Deep inside yesterday's range micro-break fade: Far from both of yesterday's extremes (more than k typical 30-minute moves from each), no reference level or stop pool is nearby to pull price; a short-term break there has nothing to run to and is absorbed by traders working inside yesterday's accepted range, so fade it.
- E-373 Narrow box inside a wide day break fade: A recent box that is only a small share of the day's already wide range is a pause inside a market whose real edges are far away; its break triggers only local stops, while the day's larger participants trade the wider edges, so the break reverts into the box.
- E-374 Break away from the prior close when already stretched: Yesterday's settlement-close is the overnight inventory's reference; when price is already more than k typical hourly moves away from it, a further short-term break away is chased by late momentum traders while holders of the overnight inventory take profit, so fade the break back toward the prior close.
- E-375 Wide initial-balance interior micro-break fade: A first hour (or half hour) whose range already covers most of a typical day's range-to-date has set broad value early, with longer-timeframe traders active at both edges; later short-term breaks that stay inside that initial balance are noise between the edges and revert, so fade them.
- E-376 Choppy-dollar index micro-break fade: Macro and foreign flow moves the dollar with direction; when the dollar's last 30 minutes have been a path with little net progress (low efficiency), no macro flow is driving Brazil assets, so a WIN short-term break is local flow that market makers fade back into the box.
- E-377 Top-third exit rotation follow: After price has stayed in the top third of the day's (normal-or-wider) range for a while, the first close back below it shows the buyers who held it there have stopped defending; their exits and the responsive sellers rotate price toward the middle of the range, so sell the exit (mirror from the bottom third).
- E-378 Gap-in break bar fade: A short-term break that was printed by the bar's opening jump (the bar opened beyond the level, above the previous close) and then traded back against it inside the bar was made by a quote vacuum at the bar's open, not by buyers lifting offers: the buyers who chased the open are already under water within the bar and liquidity providers keep leaning on the gap, so fade the break (mirror for down-breaks).
- E-379 Neutral-day micro-break fade: Market Profile's neutral day: once the session has extended both above and below its initial balance, both the other-timeframe buyers and sellers have tried and been answered, so the day is traded responsively from both edges; a short-term break inside such a two-sided session has no initiator behind it and is faded (extension on BOTH sides, unlike the unextended or wide-IB interiors already screened).
- E-380 Developing-POC stretch fade: Today's developing point of control is the price where most of today's contracts changed hands, i.e. the price both sides currently accept; after the first hour a move several typical half-hour moves away from it is carried by a thin crowd, and the first bar closing back beyond the previous bar's extreme starts the return toward the accepted price as responsive traders lean in (a stretch from today's POC, not VWAP or yesterday's POC).
- E-381 Repeated-break fatigue fade: When the same side has already broken short-term highs several times within the last hour, each new break is bought mostly by late chasers while the early breakout buyers are taking profit into it and the stop pool above has been cleared repeatedly; the m-th same-side break inside an hour is therefore exhausted flow, so fade it on the break bar itself (mirror for lows).
- E-382 Larry Williams setup 9.3 trigger: Setup 9.3 (rising 9 EMA, two closes below a reference close, buy on the break of the last bar's high) is taught alongside 9.1/9.2 to Brazilian WIN day traders; the two-bar pullback traps short-term sellers whose stops sit just above, and the setup users' buy-stops at the same place fill together, so follow the trigger (mirror on a falling EMA).
- E-383 Raschke 80-20 reversal: Raschke/Connors' 80-20: a large bar that opened in its top fifth and closed in its bottom fifth draws momentum sellers at its close; when the next bar trades below that bar's low and closes back above it, the sell-stops under the low have been run and found no follow-through, the late sellers are trapped, so buy the reclaim (mirror for a bar that opened low and closed high).
- E-384 Turtle Soup Plus One: Raschke's Turtle Soup Plus One on intraday bars: a close below a window low that was made at least four bars earlier triggers the breakout sellers and the stops of the old low's buyers; if the very next bar closes back above that old low, the break is a failed liquidity raid and the trapped breakout sellers cover, so buy the reclaim (mirror for highs); the one-bar wait and the old-level condition differ from fading the break bar itself.
- E-385 Two-day value conflict micro-break fade: When price sits between today's session VWAP and yesterday's full-session VWAP while the two are clearly apart, the holders of today's inventory and of yesterday's inventory are on opposite sides of their cost and defend opposite edges; neither camp follows a short-term break inside that band and the other absorbs it, so fade breaks in the conflicted zone.
- E-386 Hourly Crabel stretch break follow: Crabel's stretch (the average of the smaller of open-to-high and open-to-low) measures how far price normally wanders against the eventual direction from an open; applied to each clock hour with the same hour of the previous sessions, a first close beyond hour-open +/- m stretches means this hour has gone further than noise usually takes it, so a parent order is working that way; follow the first such close in the hour.
- E-387 Same-half-hour return seasonality follow: Heston, Korajczyk and Sadka find returns persist at lags that are exact multiples of a trading day: the same half-hour keeps its sign across sessions because institutions rebalance and execute on fixed daily schedules; at the last bar before each half-hour, when that coming half-hour's mean return over the previous sessions is large relative to its dispersion, trade in its sign.
- E-388 Round-trip-hour micro-break fade: When the close is back near where it was an hour ago (net hourly change small next to its usual size at this minute), the hour's buyers and sellers have cancelled out and nobody holds a directional parent order; a short-term break of the recent high/low in that state is a local push that the two-way hour absorbs, so fade it (rolling-hour net change, not the move from the open or the VWAP slope).
- E-389 Counter-leg micro-break fade: An up-break of the recent short-term high made while the close is still in the lower part of the last two hours' range is a relief rally inside a larger down leg: short covering triggers it, while the sellers who built the two-hour leg still hold inventory and sell into the bounce; fade it (mirror for down-breaks high in the two-hour range).
- E-390 Day-after-inside-day micro-break fade: A session whose whole range stayed inside the session before it was a contraction day: other-timeframe traders did not initiate and the two-day range is the frame everyone trades; the next session's short-term breaks happen inside that balance, without new initiative behind them, and revert as responsive traders lean on them.
- E-391 Double-tested box break fade: A recent box whose top AND bottom were each touched at least twice is a rectangle that both sides defend with resting orders; the first close through one edge mostly fills the stops parked just beyond it, after which the defenders who held the edge twice re-establish it, so the break reverts into the box.
- E-392 Volatile-tape micro-break fade (liquidity-provision premium): Nagel: the return to providing liquidity (short-term reversal) rises sharply when volatility is high, because intermediaries withdraw and the remaining liquidity is priced dearly; when the last half hour's range is far above its usual size at this minute, short-term breaks are demand for immediacy into a thin book, so fading them earns that elevated premium.
- E-393 Ease-of-Movement threshold cross follow: Arms' Ease of Movement measures how far the bar midpoint moves per contract traded; when its smoothed value crosses a clear threshold, price is rising (falling) with little volume needed, i.e. the opposite side has stepped back from the book; while that withdrawal lasts the move continues, so follow the cross.
- E-394 Doji-regime micro-break fade: When recent sessions have mostly closed near where they opened (small average daily body versus daily range), each day's excursions are given back by the close: the market is in a multi-day regime where intraday pushes are taken back by responsive traders; short-term breaks in such a regime are faded (daily open-to-close body share, not daily return autocorrelation or today's own candle).
- E-395 Opening half-hour micro-break fade: In the first minutes after the open the book is still being rebuilt after the auction and liquidity is thinnest, so overnight orders arriving piecemeal push small boxes back and forth; a short-term break in that window is order-arrival noise that the market makers re-quoting the book pull back, so fade it until the opening period is over.
- E-396 Snap-back break fade: A close through the recent high printed right after a clearly down bar is a V-shaped snap: the break comes from a one-bar reversal of a selling bar (stops of the dip sellers plus a burst of market buys), not from a build-up of buyers; the urgent orders are spent within the bar and the box's sellers return, so fade it (mirror after an up bar).
- E-397 Chaikin oscillator threshold cross follow: The Chaikin oscillator (fast minus slow EMA of today's accumulation/distribution line) turns when volume starts closing bars on the other side of their ranges; a cross beyond a clear threshold means accumulation (distribution) is accelerating, a parent order being worked through the session, which keeps pushing for a while, so follow it.
- E-398 Mirror-of-open swing fade: Day traders anchor on the session open; once the day has dipped well below it, the price an equal distance above the open (the low mirrored through the open) is where those who bought the low book a symmetric profit and open-anchored sellers lean, so a bar that tags the mirror level and closes back below it is faded (and the reverse after a deep high).
- E-399 Connors RSI extreme fade: A composite of very short RSI, the RSI of the up/down close streak and the percentile of the last bar's return flags moments when one side has hit the tape several bars in a row with unusually large prints; liquidity providers absorb that one-sided flow and are paid when it pauses, so fade the moment the composite enters its extreme.
- E-400 Quarter-hour block overreaction fade: Execution algorithms slice parent orders on a fixed clock; a block of 10 to 20 minutes that moved much more than usual for its time and closed at its own extreme marks a slice that pushed through thin liquidity, and short-horizon returns tend to reverse at that horizon, so fade the block at its close.
- E-401 VWAP-TWAP gap follow: When the session's volume-weighted price moves clearly above its time-weighted price, contracts are changing hands mostly at the higher prices of the day, meaning buyers accepted them with size rather than the price merely drifting there; follow the side where the volume has been transacted.
- E-402 Previous clock-hour floor-pivot fade: Floor pivots built from the previous clock hour's high, low and close give R1/S1 (or R2/S2) levels that short-term desks quote and rest orders at; a bar that tags the level and closes back inside is met by those resting responsive orders, so fade it back toward the hour's pivot.
- E-403 First box break after a quiet spell fade: When no close has escaped the rolling short-term box for a long time the tape is in balance and resting stops accumulate just outside it; the first break after such a spell is usually those stops being run by a small push, after which the balancing participants step back in, so fade it.
- E-404 Klinger volume-force hook fade: The Klinger oscillator measures signed volume force; once it has reached an extreme for this market and then turns back inside the band, the aggressive volume that drove the move is spent and the counterparties who absorbed it now hold the better inventory, so fade the move at the hook.
- E-405 Laguerre RSI hook fade: Ehlers' Laguerre RSI reacts quickly with little lag; a hook back out of its oversold (overbought) zone shows the short burst of selling (buying) has stopped making progress, and the market makers who absorbed it lean the other way, so trade the hook against the burst.
- E-406 Two-horizon range-position mismatch fade: A close at the top of the last 15 to 60 minutes while still in the lower part of a wide day's range is a short-covering bounce inside a weak session: the participants who sold higher still hold the inventory above and sell into it, so fade the local extreme toward the day's side (and the reverse on strong days).
- E-407 Ultimate Oscillator hook fade: Williams' Ultimate Oscillator blends buying pressure over three horizons, so an extreme reading means every horizon agrees the move was one-sided; when it turns back out of the extreme the multi-horizon pressure is fading and the absorbing side gets paid, so trade the hook against the move.
- E-408 Five-minute candle-close painting fade: Many WIN chart traders and bots read 5-minute candles, so the final minute of each 5-minute block is where short-term players push the price to paint a breakout close; a 1-minute close through the recent box printed exactly in that minute is cosmetic demand, not a build-up of orders, and liquidity providers take it back once the new block opens, so fade it.
- E-409 Yesterday's closing-hour range edge fade: The high and low of yesterday's last trading window bound where the closing flows (index funds, day-trade flattening, settlement hedgers) were matched; those participants rest orders at the edges the next day, so a bar that pokes beyond either edge and closes back inside meets that resting inventory, and price rotates back into the closing range; fade the poke.
- E-410 Wide-CPR day micro-break fade: The central pivot range (pivot vs. high-low midpoint from yesterday) is wide when yesterday closed far from its middle; pivot traders read a wide CPR as a sideways day and quote both sides; on such days short-term box breaks hit passive traders who expect rotation, so the break fades back into the box.
- E-411 Equity-specific day box-break follow: When the index's range so far is large next to the dollar's range (compared with the usual ratio at this minute), the day's moves come from equity-specific information, not FX flow; informed equity orders are split and persistent, so a box break on such a day has informed flow behind it; follow it.
- E-412 Stalled-break fade: A close through the recent box followed at once by a bar that makes no further extreme and closes weaker, while still outside the box, shows the breakout buyers did not get any follow-through: the stops are used up and nobody new is paying up; the late breakout entrants exit before the box defenders even need to act, so fade the stall (before it fails back inside).
- E-413 Lunch-anchored VWAP touch hold: The VWAP anchored at the B3 midday lull is the average cost of everyone who positioned for the afternoon (European close, US session flow); when price returns to it from one side and closes back on that side, that cohort defends its break-even, so trade away from the anchored level.
- E-414 Away-from-yesterday day box-break follow: When little of today's range overlaps yesterday's range, the market has left yesterday's value and is looking for new participants (initiative, not responsive, trade); box breaks in the direction away from yesterday are with the initiative side, whose orders keep arriving, so follow only those breaks.
- E-415 Dominant-volume bar side box-break follow: The bar with the most abnormal volume of the session so far (versus its time of day) marks where the day's largest participant acted, and its colour shows its side; that participant usually still has inventory to work, so box breaks in the same direction have it behind them; follow only those breaks.
- E-416 Previous clock-hour extreme close-through fade: The high and low of the hour that just finished are the stops and breakout alerts of hourly-candle traders; when that hour was not unusually wide, the first close beyond its extreme is mostly those mechanical orders firing together, after which the hour's range players lean against it; fade the close.
- E-417 Multi-day close-location giveback regime fade: When the last five sessions mostly closed near their lows, rallies have been sold into every day by holders using strength to reduce; that supply is still there, so an upward box break is sold into again and fades (mirror when sessions keep closing near their highs and dips are bought).
- E-418 Cheap-move (high price impact per contract) micro-break fade: When the recent price change per contract traded (Amihud impact) is high for the time of day, the book is thin and a few small market orders move the price a lot; a short-box break made then costs the pushers little and carries no conviction, and the liquidity providers who refill the book lean against it, so fade the break back into the box.
- E-419 Yesterday settled at its VWAP micro-break fade: When yesterday closed near its own volume-weighted price, the day's buyers and sellers ended near break-even with no trapped inventory to unwind today; with no carry-over pressure, short-term box breaks are not fed by forced exits and the two-sided players fade them back into the box.
- E-420 Failed-opening-drive day micro-break fade: When the price is back on the other side of the session open from the direction of the opening drive, the early initiative traders have been proved wrong and the day becomes two-sided: they exit into strength and weakness while responsive traders re-centre; short-box breaks on such days are absorbed, so fade them.
- E-421 Dollar parked mid-box index break fade: If the mini dollar sits in the middle of its own recent box when the index breaks its box, there is no macro or foreign-flow push behind the index move (foreign flows move both markets at once); the break is local order-book noise that index market makers take back, so fade it.
- E-422 Yesterday's range-midpoint poke fade: The midpoint of yesterday's high-low range is the fair price of yesterday's auction, and range traders rest orders there; a bar that comes from one side, trades through the midpoint and closes back on its side shows the resting orders absorbed the push, so trade away from the level.
- E-423 Inside-morning-range afternoon micro-break fade: After the morning has set the session's working range, afternoon participants (lighter local flow before the US session) trade inside it against its edges; a short-box break that still sits inside the morning range is a rotation, not a new leg, and the morning-range players fade it.
- E-424 Futures-only evening micro-break fade: Once the B3 cash-equity session and the settlement window are over, the index future trades without spot arbitrage flow or index-fund orders; what is left is day traders squaring positions in a thin book, so short-box breaks then are inventory noise that reverts; fade them.
- E-425 Rotating-box micro-break fade: A box whose closes keep crossing its own midpoint is a two-sided auction in which both sides keep being filled; a break out of such a box is usually the last swing of the rotation hitting stops just outside, after which the rotation resumes, so fade the break.
- E-426 Braided-EMA micro-break fade: When the 9- and 21-bar EMAs have crossed back and forth several times recently, the moving-average trend followers have been whipsawed and stopped repeatedly and nobody holds a trend position; a 15-minute box break then has no trend money behind it and fades back into the box.
- E-427 Same-sign index and dollar day micro-break fade: The index and the dollar usually move in opposite directions because foreign flows drive both; on a day when both are up (or down) since the open by a clear margin, the two markets are driven by separate local flows instead of one persistent foreign flow, so index box breaks lack a lasting push and fade.
- E-428 Index-dollar decorrelation micro-break fade: WIN and WDO normally move against each other minute by minute because foreign flow trades both; when the rolling correlation of their 1-bar returns rises to near zero or positive, that macro flow is absent and each book is run by local liquidity providers, so a 15-minute box break in WIN has no persistent initiator behind it and is leaned back; fade it.
- E-429 Absorbed break-bar fade: A close through the short box made by a bar that traded far more contracts per point of range than usual for its minute means the breakers met a large passive order that filled them without letting price run; the absorber holds the other side and the breakers are offside, so fade the break bar.
- E-430 Wick-dominated tape micro-break fade: When recent bars close near where they opened while trading both ways inside the bar (small average body share of the range), every push inside a bar is answered within the same bar by resting liquidity; a 15-minute box break in such a two-way tape is one more intrabar-style probe that gets answered, so fade it.
- E-431 Break toward the session VWAP follow: A short-box break that points toward a session VWAP lying several box heights away has room to run and a magnet ahead: benchmarked desks behind the VWAP are buyers (sellers) on the way back to value and the box's stops sit behind the break; follow only breaks in the direction of VWAP.
- E-432 Break into today's heavy-volume zone fade: A short-box break whose next price bins already traded much more volume today than the session's average bin runs into the inventory of everyone who transacted there; those holders sell their break-even on the way up (buy it on the way down), so the break stalls in the heavy zone and reverts; fade it.
- E-433 One-sided initial-balance extension break follow: When the session has extended beyond only one side of its initial balance, the other-timeframe participant has shown its hand in that direction and the opposite side has not even tested its edge; short-box breaks in the extension direction carry that initiator's continuing orders, so follow only those.
- E-434 Multi-day streak break fade: After one or more consecutive down (up) daily closes, short-term holders are already loaded with the multi-day move and swing traders look for the bounce (short-horizon daily reversal); intraday breaks that extend the streak's direction are the last weak hands and get bought (sold), so fade only those breaks.
- E-435 Prior-hour rejection-wick zone break fade: A completed clock hour with a long upper (lower) wick shows sellers (buyers) who rejected those prices an hour ago; a short-box break that carries the price back into that wick zone meets the same resting orders that made the wick, so fade the break while it is still inside the zone.
- E-436 Neutral-momentum break fade: A short-box break that happens while the 14-bar RSI is still near 50 is a level giving way without any accumulated one-sided pressure behind it: the buyers and sellers of the last bars are balanced, so the break is a stop poke rather than a move, and the balanced book takes it back; fade it.
- E-437 Even-volume tape micro-break fade: When bar volumes over the last window are much more even than usual (low coefficient of variation versus the previous sessions), trade is coming from steady market makers and time-sliced algos with no bursts of informed size; a 15-minute box break in that tape has no one-off initiator behind it, so fade it.
- E-438 Low-kurtosis tape micro-break fade: When the recent 1-bar price changes are thin-tailed compared with the previous sessions (low excess kurtosis), the tape is made of small two-way trades with no jumps from informed size; a 15-minute box break in that tape is a drift of liquidity providers' quotes rather than news, and they lean it back, so fade it.
- E-439 Outsized break-bar fade: A break of the short box made by a single bar whose own range is a large share of (or bigger than) the whole box spends the move in one burst of market orders that runs the stops behind the edge; once those stops are filled no initiator is left and the stop-run sellers (buyers) are the other side, so fade the break bar.
- E-440 Unclustered-volatility micro-break fade: Volatility normally clusters: a wide bar is followed by another when information arrives. When the lag-1 correlation of bar ranges in the recent window is low, ranges are random noise rather than an information flow, so a 15-minute box break carries no news and the passive side takes it back; fade it.
- E-441 Close-box break inside the wick box fade: A close beyond the highest (lowest) close of the window that is still inside the window's wick high (low) only reaches prices that were already offered and rejected intrabar; the sellers who printed the wick are still there and chart traders who buy the closing break are the liquidity, so fade it.
- E-442 Even-range tape micro-break fade: When bar ranges over the window are much more uniform than usual (low coefficient of variation versus the previous sessions), price is being walked by steady quoting with no bursts; a 15-minute box break in that tape is the regular oscillation reaching an edge, not an expansion, so fade it.
- E-443 Fast-reverting deviation micro-break fade: Fit the AR(1) coefficient of the price's deviation from its rolling mean: when it is below its usual level, deviations die out faster than normal, i.e. inventory-holding market makers are actively pulling price back to the mean; a 15-minute box break in that state is a deviation they will absorb, so fade it.
- E-444 Balance-of-power exhaustion turn fade: The rolling mean of (close-open)/(high-low) measures how consistently bars close in the buyers' (sellers') favour; when a stretch of one-sided bar pressure ends (the mean falls back through its threshold), the aggressive side has finished and the late chasers are offside, so trade against the stretch on the turn.
- E-445 Cash-open price cross fade: WIN trades from 09:00 but the cash equities open at 10:00, and the price at that moment is where index arbitrage and fund rebalancing set their reference; when price returns from well away and closes through that level, the arbitrage desks defend it as a pivot, so the cross fails more often than not: fade it.
- E-446 High ordinal-entropy tape micro-break fade: Permutation entropy of the order of three consecutive closes measures how random the tape is; when it is above its usual level, no ordinal pattern (trend runs) dominates, so nobody is working a directional order and a 15-minute box break is noise that the two-sided book absorbs: fade it.
- E-447 Rising-floor box break follow: A box whose lows rose markedly in its second half while the top held (ascending-triangle shape) shows buyers lifting their bids under a fixed seller; when the close breaks the top the seller is exhausted and the stops of shorts who sold the flat top are bought, so follow the break (mirror for a falling ceiling).
- E-448 Thin-volume micro-break fade: A break of the recent low/high made on volume well below the usual for that minute has no real participation behind it; the stops it touches are the only flow and the price returns.
- E-449 Value-aligned micro-break fade: A dip to a new short-term low that still closes above the session VWAP is a pullback inside buyer control (mirror for highs below VWAP); trapped counter-trend sellers fuel the bounce.
- E-450 Overshoot-depth fade: A close beyond the recent extreme by more than a multiple of the average bar range is a stop-run overshoot, exhausting the aggressive side; fade it.
- E-451 Rolling-VWAP envelope re-entry fade: When the close comes back inside a volume-weighted rolling envelope after an excursion outside it, the excursion has been rejected and the price reverts toward the rolling value.
- E-452 Final-hour micro-break fade: In the last hour before the close, day traders flatten and breaks of the recent range lack follow-through; fade the poke beyond the range.
- E-453 Prior-close-carry dip buy: After a session that closed near its high, dips to short-term lows early on the next session meet carried-over demand (mirror after a weak close).
- E-454 Break-then-reclaim fade: A break of the recent extreme followed one bar later by a close back inside with a bar in the opposite direction confirms the failed break; enter after the confirmation.
- E-455 Rolling z-score extreme fade: A close far from its own rolling mean in standard-deviation terms is a short-lived liquidity dislocation that tends to revert.
- E-456 Beyond-opening-range micro-break fade: Once the price sits outside the opening range, a further short-term extreme is a late chase by breakout traders who are the liquidity for fade traders.
- E-457 Large-move reversal-bar fade: After a bar-window move that is large relative to the last 20 sessions' dispersion, the first bar closing against it signals that the impulsive flow has stopped; fade the move.
- E-458 Recent same-clock window drift follow: Index flows repeat at the same clock times (index arbitrage, institutional schedules); when the average move over the same clock window of the last 5 sessions is large and today's move agrees, follow it.
- E-459 Robust median/MAD stretch fade: A price far from its rolling median in mean-absolute-deviation units is stretched by a few aggressive orders, not by a shift in value; liquidity providers pull it back.
- E-460 Up-close share extreme fade: When nearly every close in a window is up and the price sits at the window high, buyers are exhausted (one-sided tape); fade the first such bar.
- E-461 Range-burst direction follow: When the short-term average bar range jumps well above the hourly average, an informed order is working; follow the colour of the bar that makes the burst start.
- E-462 Rolling-sigma shock bar fade: A single bar return far beyond the recent bar-return sigma is usually a sweep of thin liquidity that partly reverses; fade it.
- E-463 Counter-position window break fade: A break of the recent window extreme against the session's location (rally breaks near the day low, dip breaks near the day high) is a counter-trend push; the larger flow resumes.
- E-464 Jump-dominated move fade: A move driven by one outsized bar rather than steady pressure is a liquidity gap or a single order; it tends to retrace, unlike gradual moves.
- E-465 EMA-Keltner stretch fade: Price beyond an EMA by several average bar ranges is overextended relative to its own trend; sellers or buyers of the other side step in.
- E-466 US cash-open drive follow: The US cash open (10:30 or 11:30 in Sao Paulo time depending on daylight saving) brings foreign flow to the index; a larger-than-usual first move after it tends to be followed.
- E-467 Bollinger band re-entry fade: A close back inside a Bollinger band after an excursion means the push failed; mean reversion toward the band centre follows.
- E-468 Seasonal-path deviation fade: Against the typical same-minute path of the day-return over 20 sessions, a day running far from it is stretched versus what intraday flow usually does; fade it.
- E-469 Negative-autocorrelation regime fade: When the rolling lag-1 return autocorrelation is clearly negative, bar moves are bid-ask bounce and liquidity-taker overshoots; a large bar is faded by market makers.
- E-470 One-sided bar-colour fraction fade: When almost all recent bars close one colour, aggressive flow is crowded on one side and the marginal buyer or seller is spent; fade the first time the fraction reaches the level.
- E-471 Bollinger band re-entry: A close outside a rolling mean +/- m sigma band followed by a close back inside shows the push was absorbed; fade back toward the mean.
- E-472 Volatility expansion direction follow: When the short-window average range jumps well above the hour's average, new information is being priced in; follow the direction of the short window.
- E-473 EMA stretch in range units fade: A close far from its own EMA, measured in average bar ranges, is an inventory-driven stretch that mean-reverts as liquidity providers lean against it.
- E-474 Rolling-VWAP cross on heavy volume: A close across the trailing-hour volume-weighted price on heavier than usual volume marks a shift in the cost basis of recent participants; follow it.
- E-475 Wide-window Donchian midline cross: In a window wider than usual, a close crossing the high-low midpoint shows the balance point migrating; follow it.
- E-476 Prior-session midpoint reclaim: The midpoint of yesterday's range is a fair-value reference for today's participants; after trading on one side for a while, the first close across it hands control to the other side.
- E-477 Seasonal minute-of-day forward drift: If over the last 20 sessions the forward return from this minute has had a consistent sign (net drift close to its average absolute size), recurring flow (rebalancing, hedging) may repeat; trade it.
- E-478 Multi-session closing-extreme touch fade: The highest/lowest SESSION CLOSE of the last n sessions is where overnight holders last accepted price; a bar that probes that line and closes back away from it meets passive supply/demand, so fade it.
- E-479 Multi-session last-hour edge touch fade: The extremes printed in the final hour of recent sessions are where late positions were squeezed in or out; a probe of that edge that closes back is a stale-level rejection, fade it.
- E-480 Second-extreme robust edge fade: The single most extreme daily high/low of the last n sessions is often a one-off spike; the SECOND most extreme is the level the market keeps defending. A probe that closes back is fade material.
- E-481 Price-time correlation trend onset: When session price correlates almost perfectly with time over a window, the move is orderly (one-sided flow, few pullbacks); the first bar where this trend quality is reached marks a persistent program, follow it.
- E-482 Supertrend flip follow: An ATR-based trailing line flips only when price travels against the trend by several ranges, which means stops of the old trend are run and new-direction orders enter; follow the flip.
- E-483 Multi-session floor-pivot R1/S1 touch fade: Pivots built from the last n sessions' high, low and close are used by many discretionary traders; R1/S1 act as self-fulfilling turning points, so a touch that closes back is faded.
- E-484 Double-EMA slope turn follow: A double exponential average removes most lag; its slope changing sign is an early sign that short-term order flow has turned, before slower averages show it.
- E-485 Relative Vigor Index signal cross from the wrong side: RVI measures how far bars close from their open relative to their range; a cross of its smoothed signal while still on the opposite side of zero marks the first bar-shape evidence that conviction has switched.
- E-486 Adaptive-average (KAMA) cross follow: Kaufman's adaptive average hugs price when the move is efficient and flattens in noise, so a close through it with the average already sloping that way happens mainly in efficient moves; follow it.
- E-487 Average-high/low activator break: A close beyond the mean of the last n highs (or lows) means price left the area where recent supply (demand) was working; the Gann Hi-Lo activator logic says follow it.
- E-488 Multi-day channel wick poke rejection: Swing traders rest orders at the high/low of the last 5-20 sessions; a bar that reaches that edge, prints a long wick and closes against it has met that size, so fade back from the edge.
- E-489 Opening-range midpoint cross with VWAP: The midpoint of the opening range is the opening auction's balance; once the range is complete, a close that crosses it on the VWAP side shows the day's participants leaning that way, so follow.
- E-490 VWAP cross-count chop fade: When price has already crossed the VWAP several times in the last window, VWAP acts as a magnet inside a two-sided rotation; the next cross is noise that rotational traders lean against, so fade it.
- E-491 Higher-high streak end fade: After several bars in a row printing higher highs (lower lows), a bar that fails to extend and closes against the streak shows the chasing buyers (sellers) are exhausted; fade it.
- E-492 Record-range bar closing at its extreme follow: A bar with the widest range of the session so far that closes in the top (bottom) of its range is an unusually aggressive one-sided order flow that the book could not absorb; follow it.
- E-493 Historical VWAP-deviation extreme fade: Measured against the distribution of VWAP distances seen in the previous 20 sessions, a close several standard deviations from today's VWAP is a stretch intraday traders have repeatedly sold (bought); fade.
- E-494 Price-volume correlation follow: When bar price changes correlate positively with volume, up moves are traded heavily and down moves lightly: aggressive buyers dominate; follow the sign when the correlation turns strongly one way.
- E-495 Volatility-burst extreme break follow: When the average range of the last few minutes jumps well above the last hour's, new information or a large parent order is moving the market; a close beyond the burst window's extreme is followed.
- E-496 Bar-colour dominance follow: When most bars of the last window close up (down) and the net move agrees, a steady stream of small marketable orders is lifting offers (hitting bids) rather than one big print; follow it.
- E-497 Prior-session central pivot hold-cross follow: The prior session's central pivot (H+L+C)/3 is a reference many desks publish; a run of closes that stays beyond it means the day accepted the new side, so follow the first such confirmed cross.
- E-498 EMA z-score stretch fade: A close several standard deviations from its own session EMA is a liquidity-driven stretch by an impatient taker; passive liquidity should pull it back toward the mean.
- E-499 US-cash-open impulse follow: When US equities open (10:30 or 11:30 São Paulo depending on DST) foreign flow hits the index; the first minutes' direction should carry as hedgers and index arbitrageurs follow.
- E-500 Day-open recross after an excursion: After an unusually large excursion from the session open, a close back through the open means the day's initiators lost; the other side takes control.
- E-501 Prior-session midpoint cross follow: The prior session's range midpoint splits yesterday's value in two; a close across it shifts the balance and the move should extend as range traders reposition.
- E-502 Volatility-expansion direction follow: When recent bar ranges jump over the last hour's average, informed urgency has arrived; the net direction of that burst tends to carry while slower participants chase.
- E-503 Higher-high higher-low ladder follow: Consecutive bars each making higher highs and higher lows (or the reverse) show trapped counterparties covering step by step; follow the staircase.
- E-504 Opening-range midpoint cross follow: After the opening range is set, the midpoint is where early buyers and sellers balance; a close across it signals the winning side and should extend.
- E-505 Keltner channel break follow: A close beyond EMA plus a multiple of average bar range signals a volatility-adjusted breakout; stop orders and momentum followers feed the move.
- E-506 Same-minute seasonal drift follow: Scheduled flows (funds, hedgers, index rebalance, data releases) recur at the same clock minute; if the last 20 sessions showed a consistent bar direction at this minute, join it.
- E-507 Chaikin money-flow zero-zone cross follow: Where bars close inside their range, weighted by volume, reveals who is absorbing; a flip of this flow beyond a threshold shows a new aggressor.
- E-508 First box break of each clock hour fade: The first close beyond the recent range inside a clock hour is the probe of hourly-rebalancing flow against a thin book; later breaks in the hour carry more information, the first is faded by liquidity providers.
- E-509 Prior-day-candle-aligned dip/rip fade: After a prior session that closed clearly up (down), a close below (above) the recent range is a dip (rip) against yesterday's carried positioning; swing holders buy (sell) it back.
- E-510 Round-number micro-break fade: Closes that break the recent range right at a round index number run into resting limit orders and option-strike hedging at that number, so the break is faded.
- E-511 Prior-profile air-pocket follow: Price entering a price bin that traded little yesterday (low-volume node inside yesterday's range) meets little resting liquidity, so the move through it accelerates; follow the entry direction.
- E-512 Spike-away-from-the-mass break fade: When most closes of the window sit at the opposite end of the box from the break, the break is a spike by few orders, not a drift of the crowd; the mass pulls price back.
- E-513 Day-after-choppy-path break fade: After a session whose path efficiency was lower than its recent norm (much travel, little net move), participants keep rotating; breaks of short ranges are faded the next day.
- E-518 Gap-driven micro-break fade: A short-range break that was built by open-vs-previous-close gaps (aggressive market orders jumping the book) rather than by trading inside bars is a liquidity-taking spike; the book refills and price returns.
- E-519 Multi-day session-VWAP channel edge fade: The highest and lowest session VWAPs of recent sessions bound where participants agreed on value; a short-range break that reaches that edge meets inventory holders and tends to fail.
- E-520 Inside-bar-primed micro-break fade: A short-range break on the bar right after a small inside bar is a release of a coil by a few stop orders, not a conviction move; fade it.
- E-521 V-pop micro-break fade: A big bar that breaks a short-range extreme right after an opposite-colour bar is a one-bar squeeze of stops with no second wave behind it; fade the pop.
- E-522 Pocket-pivot volume follow: An up bar on more volume than every down bar of the recent window, above VWAP, shows buyers absorbing all the selling seen lately (and the mirror for sells); follow it.
- E-523 Awesome Oscillator saucer follow: In an established momentum phase (AO on one side of zero), two shrinking bars followed by a re-growing one mark a pause that ended; trade with the phase.
- E-524 Session-extreme two-bar upthrust fade: A new session high (low) followed at once by a close under its low (over its high) traps breakout buyers (sellers) at the day's edge; their exits feed the reversal.
- E-525 Time-block micro-break fade: Liquidity and participant mix change through the day; in some 90-minute blocks short-range breaks mostly fail because the flow behind them is thin.
- E-526 DeMarker extreme fade: DeMarker measures how often highs are pushed up versus lows pushed down bar to bar; a reading near an extreme means one side has been extending the range for too long to continue at the same pace.
- E-527 True Strength Index extreme fade: A double-smoothed momentum ratio at an extreme means price moved almost one-directionally for a long stretch; late followers become the supply (demand) for the reversal.
- E-528 One-sliver poke fade: A bar that pokes only a sliver beyond the previous bar's extreme and closes back against the prior close shows stop orders just beyond the extreme found no follow-through; fade the poke.
- E-529 Prior-session open recross follow: The previous session's opening price is a reference many participants remember; a close back across it after a long stay on one side flips the sentiment of yesterday's openers, follow the cross.
- E-530 Fading-volume run fade: A run of closes in one direction while each bar trades less volume than the last means the push lacks participation; liquidity providers fade it.
- E-531 Session signed-volume share cross follow: The share of the session's volume traded in up bars minus down bars summarises who has been the aggressor all day; when it crosses a threshold the day's inventory holders are on one side, follow.
- E-532 Event-minute heavy bar follow: A bar with volume far above its usual same-minute level is a scheduled-or-news event; the informed flow behind it keeps pushing in the bar's direction.
- E-533 Closing-hours VWAP-side breakout follow: In the last hours, a market that has stayed on one side of VWAP and makes a fresh short-term extreme has benchmark (VWAP) buyers or sellers still to execute before the close; follow.
- E-534 Elder-Ray power hook in an EMA trend: In a rising EMA trend, bear power (low minus EMA) turning up from its recent low marks sellers losing the pullback; buy the hook, sell the mirror in a falling trend.
- E-535 Counter-colour run on the VWAP side resumption: Several counter-trend closes that never lose the VWAP are a shallow pullback inside the side that institutions defend; the first bar back with the trend resumes it.
- E-536 Wyckoff no-supply / no-demand test: A narrow, low-volume down bar inside an uptrend (no supply) shows sellers have dried up; the mirror no-demand bar in a downtrend; trade with the trend.
- E-537 Low-overlap thrust fade: Consecutive bars barely overlapping mean one side took liquidity aggressively; such thrusts overshoot and partly revert once the urgent flow is done.
- E-538 Day-open revisit hold: After trading on one side of the session open for a while, a bar that dips back to the open and closes on the original side shows the open price defended by that side's buyers/sellers; follow the original side.
- E-539 Prior-session open rejection: The previous session's opening print is a reference few systems track; a poke into it from one side that closes back is rejected supply/demand; fade it.
- E-540 VWAP-deviation time-of-day z fade: A distance from VWAP large compared with what this minute of the day usually shows is a stretch that mean-reverting liquidity providers fade once the bar turns.
- E-541 Bar-colour share extreme fade: When nearly all recent bars have closed the same colour, aggressive flow is spent and the first bar count reaching the extreme share marks a point where the move is crowded; fade it.
- E-542 Realized-volatility surge direction follow: A jump in short-window realized volatility above this time of day's norm signals new information arriving; informed flow keeps pushing in the direction of the move that caused it.
- E-543 Midday-price cross follow: The price at a fixed midday clock mark is where the morning's positions are balanced; a held close beyond it in the afternoon shifts the balance and the new side keeps control.
